PREFACE.

out a project which he deemed of very great importance to the Lodges
in California, with a probability of considerable pecuniary loss, he has
accepted and acted upon the permission thus given. It is needless to
say that it has cost much time and labor, as with the view of arranging
the proceedings in the same order as that adopted by the writer in the two
succeeding years, nearly all of that which had previously been printed
has been re-written. The files of the Grand Secretary's office have
been thoroughly searched, and a number of papers, not published heretofore, have been introduced in their proper places. :Many grammatical and typographical errors have been corrected, and not a few
changes in form and phraseology have been made, but in no case has
there been the least alteration of any fact, opinion or idea presented in
the record; and the work is now presented to the Fraternity in California with the confident belief that in method of arrangement and
neatness of typography it will compare favorably with any similar
l\Iasonic publication.

ALEX. G. ABELL.
SAN FRANCISCO,

May I, A. L. 5857.

ARTICLE VII.
GRAND OFFICERS.
S11:cT10N I. The Most Worshipful Grand Master or the Right Worshipful
lloputy Gl;'and Master, may, either of them, upon the petition of seven or more
Master Masons, properly recommended by the nearest Lodge of Ancient York
Me.sons working under a Charter, gtant Dispensations to form new Lodges
within the limits of this State, or in the adjoining Territories where no Grand
l.od~e exists; which Dispensation shall be returnable at the next regular Com·
mumcation of the Grand Lodge, when the Grand Lodge may order a per·
pctua.l Charter, or continue the Dispensation until the next succeeding regular
meeting of the Grand Lodge.
8Ec. 2. On the first day or the regular meeting of the Grand Lodge, or as
Hoon thereafter as possible, the Grand Master shall lay before the Grand
J,odge a written message, therein detailing an account of his official acts
1luring the recess, and the state and condition of Masonry in his jurisdiction;
11nd shall recommend to the Grand Lodge such measures as he may deem
oxpedidnt and necessary.
SEO. 3. The Deputy Grll.nd Master shall also report, in writing, at every
Aomi-annual meeting of the Grand Lodge, all his acts and doings relating to
hiM offiee, during ihe vacation.
S&c. 4. The Grand Secretary, Grand Treasurc'r and Grand Tyler, shall re·
coive such compensation for their services as the Grand Lodge may, from time
to time, determine.
SEc. 5. It shall be the duty of the Grand Secretary to pay over to the Grand
'!'rec.surer all moneys in his hands belonging to the Grand Lodge, at.each regular
Communication, and quarterly, during the recess thereof; and to render an account of the same at each semi-annual meeting of the Grand Lodge.
8Ec. 6. The Grand Treasurer shall receive all moneys from the Grand Sec
retsry, belonging to the Grand Lodge, keep a correct record thereof, and, at'
each semi-annual Communication of the Grand Lodge, shall render an account
of a.II his receipts and disbursements l but no disbursement shall be made by
him without an order from the Grand Lodge, signed by the presiding officer
1rnd the Grand Secretary, excepting as hereinafter provided.
f'lEc. 7. The Grand 'frea.surer elect, prior to his installation, shall execute
io the Grand Master for the time being, to the use of the Grand Lodge, a bond,
with such ~ecurity, and for such sum, as the Grand Master may approve, con1litioned for the faithful diseharge of his duties.
SEc. 8. All other Grand Officers receiving funds belonging to the Grand
Lodge, shall immediately transmit the same to the Grand Secretary.
ARTICLE VIII.
POWERS

OF THE GRAND LODGE.

8ECTIOll. L The Grand Lodge may grant Charters for new Lodges, to he
located within the limits of its jurisdiction, to a competent nuiuber of worthy
brethren applying therefor: Provided, that no new Lodge shall be established
hy Dispensation or otherwise, excepting upon the recommendation of the Lodge
nearest to the. place of the location of the proposed new Lodge : And provided

cnce; and, when so convened, to hold an election of officers; whereupon he
shall proceed to constitute said Lodge, and install such officers, according to
the ancient regulations and usages of the craft.

and regulations as it may deem necessary, not inconsistent with this Consti·
tution and the ancient rules and regulations of Freemasonry.

Any proposed amendment or alteration of this Constitution may
be presented, in writing, to the Grand Lodge at a semi-annual meeting, by the

of the members present, it 11hall be published with the minutes as a subject for
future action ; and upon its adoption n t the next regular Communication of
the Grand Lodge, by a vote of two thirds of the members present, the same
shall become a part of this Constitution.

The following communication was Teceived from the M.·. W.-. Grand
Master, which, having been read, was ordered to be entered upon the
minutes: .7b the JI.·, W.·. Grand Lodge qf Oalifornia:
BRETHUN: The formation of a Grand Lodge for the State of California
should be a subject of sincere congratulation to every Mason here and elsewhere· for probably in no part of the habitable globe has its usefulness been
made ~o manifest as in this country within the past year. Already does California number amona its inhabitants persons of every nation and clime known
to the civilized world~ all seeking, by enterprise and industry, to improve their
condition in life; and it not unfrequently happens that within the distance of
a. few hundred yards of a mining di~trict, there will be fou:id ma.ny person.s
laboring torrether who a.re strangers m language, heart., and m feehngs; but 1f
they be mei':ibers of our ancient Order, each is the master of a language that all
can understand, and there ~t pnce springs up th?t. deei;> feeling of fraternal.
affection which has eyer so d1stmctly marked and d1stmgmshed .ilie members of
the Masonic Fraternity abo_ve all others; for, as a mem b~r of this Order, n_o ?ne
can be a strancrer among his brethren - no one can be m sickness or affliction
without some ;ne ministerinu to his wants and contriouting to his comfortsa.nd no where is thie holy felfowship and brotherly love more frequently called
into requisition than in this land of gold and promise; for no where is man
more dependent upon his fellow man for a~l th? necessary comforts of !ife than
in California. · and so often has the bemgn mf!nence of the Masomc Order
been witness~d and felt in various parts of this country, as well in he11.lt!J as in
the dreary hours of sickness and death, that all who have witnessed those influences have learned properly to appreciate the benefits of the Institntion in a
community like ours. 'fhe cnltivation of brotherly love and affection is therefore earnestly recommended to all, and especially among the Lodges of this
jurisdiction. No strife '.should ever exist, either among Masons or Lodges,
except that honorable strife or emul~tion of who can bes~ p~omote the happiness of his fellow man, and the true interest, honor, and d1gmty of our anment
Craft.
In accordance with the requirements of the Constitution of the Grand Lodge,
the Grand Master respectfully reports, that since the adjournment of the Grand
Lodge he has pursuant to a. resolution passed April nineteenth, A. L. 5850,
issued 'a Chart~r to Berryman Lodge, located in Sacramento City ; and that he
has also examined the books of Benicia Lodge, and finding their work to have
been conducted according to the usages of the Order, has issued .a Charte~ to
that Lodge· under which Charters both Lodges are now workmg. Owmg
to the short' time that has elapsed since the orge.nfaation of the Grand Lodge,
the Grand Master has no other official acts to report at its present Communi·
cation.
From the best information that can be obtained on tlie sn bject, there can
be no doubt that Masonry is in a most flourishing condition in this country. In
no part of the world is its usefulness to society more apparent than in California and hence the manifest desire ofso many persons to be admitted to the
myste~ies of the Order. ID; the Ci~y of San Francisco this i~ esp~cially the
case· for at this time there 1s standmg upon the books of California Lodge a
greater number of candidates f~r each d~gree than the Constitutio? au~hori.zes
to be admitted at any one meetmg, and, m consequence, prepe.rat10n 1s bemg
ma.de for the establishment of one or more other Lodges in that City.
The Lodges under the jurisdiction of this Grand J:odge, having received t~eir
original Dispensations and Charters from several ddferent States of the U mon,
have not that uniform system of work which should characterize the Lodges

[4]

1i~50.]

29

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA.

within the same jurisdiction. The Grand }faster would therefore recommend
I.hat a committee be appointed, denominated a Working Committee, who shall
meet, compare their knowledge, and report to the Grand Lodge at its next
meetmg, the proper mode of work for the Lodges nuder its jurisdiction.
In all action the Grand Lodge may deem proper to take upon any matter
that will tend to promote the usefulness, interest, or honor of the Masonic Order
i 11 California, the Grand Master promises his most cordial cooperation.
J. D. STEVENSON, Grand Master.

The following communication was received from the Grand Master:SAN FRANCISCO, Not', 25th, A. L. 5850.

To the N.·. W:. Grand Lodge of California: .It is with inexpr~ssible regret that the Grand Master finds himself deprived by
circumstances which he can not control, of the pleasure of meeting the Grand
Lodge at its present semi-annual Communication; but he beers to assure bis
brethren that in every feeling of the heart, as well as in interest~he is with them,
and will take pleasure in carrying into effect any action they may take upon
any subject calculated to promote the true interest of the Masonic fraternity,
here or elsewhere.
Pursuant to the requirements of the Constitution, the Grand Master has the
honor to report, that since the last semi-annual Communication, Dispensations
have been granted by him for four Lodgeg, to wit: Davy Crockett Lodi;c,
located in San Francisco; San Jose Lodge, located in San Jose; 'l'ualum1ie
Lodge, located at Sonora ; Willamette Lodge, located at the town of Portland,
9regon Tei_:ritory; all of which are in .a flourishin15 condition, and nre promot~
mg, by their usefulness, the best feelmg of respect in the communitv for the
Order. In addition to these, applications for three more Charters from different
points in California and Oregon are on their way, and may reach the Grand
Lodge before the close of the present session. At the next semi-annual Communication of the Grand Lodge, there can be no doubt of a great accession of
Lodges, and that tlrn usefulness and strength of the fraternal bonds that bind
Masons to each other, will be made so manifest as to secure to the Order the
confidence and respect of the whole community. At every point where Lod"'cs
are located, the good influences of the Institution are seen and felt, and the
manifest desire to become members is greater than the capacities of the Lodges
to admit them. Under these circumstances, every Mason feels a just pride in
acknowledging himself a member of our ancient and honorable Order.
The Grand Master would again renew his suggestion, that some action be
taken upon the subject of establishing a uniform system of work for the several Lodges under the jurisdiction of this Grand Lodge, if it be practicable·
or that, at least, proper Grand Visitants be appointed, whose duty it shall bet~
visit each Lodge within the State, and report their condition.
'fhe Grand Master would ca.II the attention of the fraternitv to tke fact, that
the ~irand Lo?ge is still without any regalia, or the necessary implements and
furmture reqmred by the well-established regulations of the Order. He would
therefore recommend, if the funds will justify it, that a committee be appointed to procure suitable regalia, implements, and furniture, or so much
thereof as m11y be deemed most necessary for the use of the Grand Lodae.
J. D. STEVENSON, Grand Mast~r.

{

Bro. B. Jennings,
" N. 0. Hinman,
'' L. B. Mizner.

The following communication, accompanying a handsomely bound
copy of the "]free Mason's Monitor" was received, and a vote of thanks
was tendered to Bao. J. L. CRoss, for his appropriate and acceptable
present: NEW YoRK1 September I2th 1 .A. L. 5850.
'lli the M:. W:. Grand Lodge of Oalifoniia : I ta~e the liberty of presenting to your Grand Body a copy of the true
Masomc Chart, or Hieroglyphic Monitor, a. work which has been sanctioned
rt8 a text-book, by most of the Grand Lodges of these United Stateii, and very
i.:enerally used by.all the members of the subordinate Lodges for the past thirty
years. I would take this occasion for expressing to you my great pleasure at
1he rece~t act of Congress, in admitting your golden State into the Union,
l,hus addmg .one more link to our beautiful chain; and for expressing the
hope that umon, harmony, and brotherly love may continue to be our wa.tchword~, which no sectional interest may ever sever.
A list of your Grand Officers, together with a list of your subordinate Lodges,
would be very acceptable.
I arn, with esteem, yours fraternally.
JEREMY L. CROSS.

the IXth Article of the C~nstitution, presented the following report : To the M:. W. ·. Grand Lodge of Oalifm·nia: The committee to whom was referred Article IX. of the Constitution, beg
leave to recommend that it be altered so as to read as follows:ARTICLE IX.
REVENUE.

SECTION. 1. The fee for instituting a subordinate Lodge shall b: one hundred and fifty dollars one hundred dollars of which shall be paid upon the
issuing of the Disp~mm.tion, and fiftl ~ollars when a Charter shall have been

41

l(rllnted; but upon the issuing of a Charter, in a case where no previous
lll•11ensation shall have been granted, the whole amount of one hundred and
llll,Y dollars shall be required. Ancl every Lodge, upon receipt of its Charter,
Mh111l pay the Grand Secretary the sum of twenty.five dollars for engrossing
tho same, and affixing thereto the seal of the Grand LodO'e.
~
Si:u. 2. Each Lodge shall pay into the treasury of the Grand Lodge the
•uni of one dollar and fifty cents for every degree conferred upon candidates,
I.ho aurn of two dollars for every adjoining member, and the sum of two dolh1rH, semi-annually, for each contributing member.
Hi.:u. 3. There shall be paid into the hands of the Grand Secretary, for the
llM!I of the Grand Lodge, five dollars, by the applicant, for every Diplom.a.
1trm1ted to a member of a subordinate Lodge; but no Diploma shall issue ex·
c•opt upon the presentation of a certificate, from the Secretary of the Lodge Qf
which the applicant is a member, of his good and regular standing therein.
H1wh Diploma, when issued, shall be signed by the Grand Master, or Deputy

[November,

SACRAMENTO CITY1 Nov.

21th, 1850.

To the M:. W:·. Grand Lodge of <Jalifornia: An ample provision for the education of children has ever been one of the
dearest objects of every good Mason.
The thousands of Masons coming to this country who die on their way, and
the thousands whp will die and have died after arrivin~ here, warn us that
some provision should be made for the education of their orphans ; and tpe
sooner we commence the work of preparation, the more apt we will be to suc·
ceed to our own satisfaction.
I own four hundred acres of high and beautiful land within one mile of this
city, about ten acres of which I design laying off in its centre, for the use
of a Masonic Institution of Learning; the balance I design laying off into
family squares, that residences may be had near enough to the city for bbsiness, and at the same time, our children can be educated at home.
I propose to give to the Grand Lodge the ten acres, and also, five per cent.
of the balance of the tract; and ask that during this session they will appoint
trustees to receive the deed for the property.
Yours fraternally,

A. M. WINN.

Bro. WM. G. DEAL presented the following communication: -

To the M. ·. W. ·. Grand Lodge of California : One year ago the Masons of this city united with the Odd Fellows to provide
a place for the indigent sick. Until within a few wee~s, the hospital o_f th.ese
two Orders has had in its wards from five to twenty sick. For defraymg the
expense we have depended on voluntary contributions, but the burthen has
rallen upon comparatively few.
The total amount of indebtedness which has been incurred to
the present time is .......................................................... $31,,!3G 14
The 11mount of above which has been paid is ..................... 17,010 70
Leaving an indebtedness existing of.. ............................... $14,425 44
For a large portion of this the Trustees are sued, and for the payment of the
whole the credit and honor of our Brotherhood here are pledged. ·we therefore
ask th~ Grand Lodge to take our situation into consideration, _and ~uthor_ize
two persons, in the States, to appeal to our brethren there to aid us m reheying ourselves from embarrassment, that we may be prepared to go forward m
that good work of relief, in which the Order has acted so noble a part within
the past year.
WM. GROVE DEAL, Pres., I
JORN F. MoRsE, Sec.,JIf
Board of 11rustees.
N.
CUNNINGHAM,
A. M. WINN,
J. BIGLER,

report:'lb tlte Jll·. W.·. Grand Lodge of California: -·
'l'he· committee appointed to examine a11d correct the reports of the various
I,odges, and to make such suggestions, and recommend such alterations as to
t.hcm might seem expedient, have had the same under consideration, and
would respectfully report as follows :

[15]

48

[November,

JOURNAL OF PROCEEDINGS.

They have had under consideration the subject of taxation by the Grand
Lodge, and its power to tax subordinate Lodges, and are of the unanimous
opinion that the Grand Lodge has not the power to tax Lodges working under
Dispensation; and thus, no dues having accrued to the Grand Lodge, under
the 2d Section of Article IX. of the Constitution, from the Lodges working
under Dispensation, they report only the fees due from such Lodges for their
Dispensations and Charters.
The committee have carefully examined and corrected the reports of the
various Lodges working under Charters, so far as in their power, from the
dates and information contained in said reports, and present the amount of
fees and dues which have accrued to this Grand Lodge under the !lth Article
of the Constitution. Many of the reports being very imperfect, for want of
dates and other necessary infor.-::J.tion, they would suggest to the various
Lodges the imperative necessity of a full and complete report, with the date
of all admissions, of conferring e:i.ch degree, and of every demit, resignation,
suspension, expulsion, death, or other matter of interest to the Grand Lodge.
The committee report the following amount of dues, and of fees for Charters
and Dispensations, as having accrued from the various Lodges, from the organization of this Grand Lodge, to Nov. 1st, 58.50, to wit:

63

'l'ho Grand Master then delivered the following Address : 711 lite M:. W:. Grand Lodge of California : 'l'htl Grand Master, pursuant to the provisions of the Constitution of the Grand
J,rnlge, respectfully reports the condition of Masonry in California and in
Ol'lll-(On, (whose Lodges are under this jurisdiction,) to be as prosperous and
1•1·rnlitable to the Order, as it can be in any part of the world. But one year
h11H elapsed since the organization of the Grand Lodge of California. At that
ll11m there were but three chartered Lodges in this State, and but two of those
wn1·u properly organized, and in a working condition. The others, who formed
11 portion of the Convention, were working under Dispensation. Since that time
11m11y Lodges have been organized by Charter and Dispensation, here and in
t h•1•:.;on, all of which are prosperous and flourishing. It should be a source
of ~i11cere and heartfelt pleasure to every true-hearted Mason, to know and feel
Jlmt. our Institution has been productive of so much good in this new country, nnd that the members of the fraternity here are so numerous and respect·
nhln; for it is a well established fact, that every arrival, whether from Europe,
or from any part of the Union, brings to us Masons of the highest character,
r11Hpectability, and usefulness. The great benefit of the Order to the human
li1111ily, has been fully proven in California, as it has been demonstr!l.ted that
uo where have so many sick and distressed brethren, within the same space
of' time, received so much fraternal relief and brotherly attention, as in Cali·
for11ia.
'!'he Grand Master would again suggest the necessity of adopting some
11w1111s whereby a uniform system of work may be established among the Lodges
under this jurisdiction. 'l'o perfect such a system, will require muck time,
lnhor, and no inconsiderable expense ; yet the great benefits which will result
Umrefrom, must be so apparent to all, that no effort should be spared to ac·
umn plish it.
I nm advised of meetings having been held at various points in California
111111 Oregon for the purpose of forming new Lodges ; and in this city, but for
lho nwful calamity which has just visited us, a Charter for a Lodge of our
l"rench brethren would have been asked for at the present Communicafam, as
llll the necessary :.rrangements and recommendations had been made, to enahlo them to make the application in due form.
Communications have been received from the Grand Lodges of Maine,
Now Hampshire, Vermont, Hhode Island, New York, Maryland, District of
llolumhia, Virginia, North Carolina, Indiana, Mississippi, and Florida, and
1l1wlld in the hands of the Committee on Foreign Correspondence, who will,
u ;luc time, mitlrn the necessary report thereon.
It is to be regretted that schism still exists in some of the States, and esmcinlly in the State of New York, where two parties claim each to be the
ugitimate Grand Lodge. However painful it may be to us, as .Masons, to
witness this disagreement, I regard it as so peculiarly a local question, which
1um only be settled by the fraternity of that State, that I deem it my duly to
"11g:.;cst to the Grand Lodge of California the avoidance of any expression
upon the subject of the legal right of either party to be considered the legitiniu.te Grand Lodge, until they shall have settled the difficulty among them-

On motion of BRO. T. A. THOMAS, the report of the Grand Secretary was referred to the Committee on ]finance and Accounts.
The Deputy Grand Master presented the following report : -

To tlte l'rl. '. W:·. Grand Lodge of Oalifornia:
Since the last Communication of this Grand Lodge, I have granted Dispen·
sations to La Fayette Lodge, at Nevada City, and to Eureka Lodge, at Gray
Eagle; the petitions for each ha'Ving been recommended by Lodges in Sacramento City. La Fayette Lodge was organized and working under a Charter
from the Grand Lodge of Wisconsin until the destruction of Nevada City by
fire in March last, when the Charter was lost, leaving a great deal of unfinished work on band, which I considered a good and sufficient reason for granting a Dispensation. The Dispensation of Eureka Lodge was returned to
me without any organization, the brethren not having had time to effect one,
previous to this Communication of the Grand Lodge. It is the wish of the
brethren to have the Dispensation continued until the next Communication.
J. A. TUTT, Deputy Grand Ma<tter.

To amend Article VIII., so as to read :
"POWERS OF THE GRAND t,ODGE.

SECTION, 1. The Grand Lodge may grant Charters for new Lodges, to be
located within the limits of its jurisdiction, to a competent number of worthy
brethren applying: Provided, that no new Lodge shall be established by
Dispensation or otherwise, except upon the recommendation of the Lodge
nearest or most convenient to the place of the proposed new Lodge. And provided further, that in case there are two or more Lodges in the same town or
place nearest or most convenient to said proposed location, the recommenda·
tion of either of them shall be deemed sufficient.
SEc. 2. The Grand Lodge shall have full and complete appellant and corrective powers in all cases relative to the fraternity;within its jurisdiction; and
it shall have power to assess upon its subordinate Lodges such contribu·
tions 1 from time to time, as the good of the Order may require."
Your committee recommend that Section 3, of this Article, be stricken out.
To a.mend Article IX., so as to read :
"REVENUE,
SECTION. 1. The fee for instituting a subordinate Lodge shall be one h1mdred and twenty-five dollars, seventy-five dollars of which shall be paid on
the issuing of a Dispensation, and fifty dollars when the Charter is granted ;
hut upon the issuing of a Charter, in a case where no previous Dispensation
shall have been granted, the whole amount of one hundred and twenty.five
dollars shall be required. Ana every Lodge, upon receipt of its Charter,
shall pay to the Grand Secretary the sum of twenty-five dollars for engrossing
the same, and affixing thereto the seal of the Grand Lodge.
SEc. 2. Each Lodge shall pay into the treasury of the Grand Lodge the
sum of one dollar for every degree conferred upon candidates, the sum of one
dollar for every adjoining member, and the sum of one dollar, semi·aunually, for each contributing member!'
Your committee recommend that Sections 3 and 4, of this Article, be re·
tained.
To amend Article X., so as to read: " SP!ORDINATE LODGES.

SECTION. 1. Every subordinate Lodge shall elect its officers annually, by
ballot, by a majority of the votes of the members present, at the stated meeting
next preceding the anniversary of St. John the Evangelist, (except in cases
hereinafter provided,) who shall be installed on the evening of their election,
or as soon thereafter as practicable, and shall retain their respective offices
until their successors in office shall be elected and duly installed."
Your committee recommend that Sections 2, 3, and 4, of this Article, be
retained, and that Sections 5 and 6 be amended so as to read: "SEC. 5. No Lodge shall confer the three degrees in Masonry for a less
sum than seventy·five dollars ; and the fee for each degree, as may be reg·
ulated by the Lodge, shall be invariably paid in advance.
SEc. 6. All applications for initiation or membership shall be made in
writing, at a stated meeting of the Lodge ; but shall not be acted upon until
after the expiration of two weeks, and then only at a regular meeting. Nor
shall there be conferred more than one degree upon the same candidate at
any one meeting of the Lodge; and no Lodge shall confer the degrees upon
more than five candidates at any one meeting."

t.he Grand Master, as relates to the establishment of a uniform system of
work for the Lodges under the jurisdiction of this Grand Lodge, have duly
eonsidered the subject, and respectfully report: That they can suggest 110 mode for accomplishing the ebject, admitted on
1dl accou 11ts to be most desirable and necessary, but by oral instruction to be
delivered by lectures to the several subordinate Lodges, from Grand Lecturcirs duly appointed and approved by the Grand Lodge; and, inasmuch as the
field to be traveled over is so broad in its geographical extent, as to render
tho visitatio11 of the several Lodges almost impossible to Bllj' one Lecturer,
your committee recommend the nominatiun and appointment, at the present
HCJHsion, of three Grand Lecturers, who will be expected to agree upon a sys·
I.em of work to be communicated by them in their lectures, and to divide the
Lodges under this jurisdiction to suit the convenience of their visitations;
1111d also to notify the several Lodges of the time and terms upon which lectures may be had by all Lodges desirous of receiving them.
Your committee would suggest thQt such compensation for traveling exp1mses and time, as may be satisfactory to the several Lecturers, be paid by
thn Lodges which may receive the benefit of lectures, so that no charge shall
Ii.ill upon the Grand Lodge.
All of which is respectfully submitted.

Bro. J. D. STEVENSON offered the following preamble and resolution:
WHEREAS, Several of the Lodges under this jurisdiction, and the members

thereof, by their great liberality to stranger brethren, sick and in distress, have
become indebted in greater amounts than they are able to pay; therefore Resolved, That a committee of seven (of which the Grand Master to be
chairman) be appointed to address a circular to other Grand and subordinate
Lodges, setting forth the amount and nature of the embarrassments aforesaid,
for what causes incurred, and the amount and nature of the relief afforded, etc.;
which circular shall be published with the proceedings of this Grand.Lodge.

CIRCULAR ADDRESS.

To the .V.·. W.·. Grand Masters, Wardens, and Brethren oftlie Grand Lodges
of the United States and of Europe, and to the W.·. Masters, Officers,
and Bretht·en of the subordinate Lodges under their respective jurisdic·
tions: At the Annual Communicatiou of the
W.-. Grand Lodge of the State
of California, the following resolution was unanimously adopted : -

.Y.-.

"WHEREAS, Several of tbe Lodges under this jurisdiction, and the members thereof,
by their great liberality to their stranger brethren, have become indebted for gr.,ater
amounts than they are able to pay; therefore" Resolved, That a committee of seven be appointed to address a circular to other
llrand and subordinate Lodges, setting forth the amount and nature of the embarrassments aforesaid, and for what causes incurred, which circular shall be published with the
proceedings of this Grand Lodge."

'fhe undersigned were appointed that committee, and in the discharge of
their duties, beg your indulgence to state : That at an early period in the history of the immense emigration into this new country from every State in the
Union, and from every part of Europe, it was found that vast numbers came
without any adequate knowledge ·of the nature or extent of the wants to which
they would become exposed in the prosecution of the mining enterprises which
they came to pursue, as well as without any idt>a of the causes which have
been found to opcrat>: in breaking down their health and constitutions. They
came most lamentably unsupplied with any other me:ins of providing for their
necessities than their capacities for labor. It therefore happened in thfl autumn
of 184,9, and the succeeding winter, that large numbers were compelled to
leave the mining distril'ts, and repair to Sacramento City for medical advice,
and for attendance in sickness. As a considerable number of such sick and destitute persons were memb.irs of the Masonic Fraternity, they naturally and of
right made their condition known to their brethren, then established at that
place.
Under a most generous impulse, and a lively sense of their obligations, the
brethren undertook to institute a sanitary establishment, that should supply the
place of a hospital. At that time, munici1,>al government had not been organized, and public affairs were conducted chiefly on the voluntary principle. So
it was in the case of the health establishment set up by the Masons. They found,
however, that the demands upon them were, in the course of a few months, increased to an extent greater than they had anticipated, and greaterthan their
own means, and the contributions which they could collect, would enable
them to meet. They could not, however, go back, nor could they abandon
the sufferers whom they had been obliged to receive, and whose numbers
were greatly multiplied by the distress caused by the freshet which swept over
the City in the winter of 1849·'50. As a consequence, they made themselves
liable for an amount of bills, the payment of which would reduce some of
them to absolute indigence. They raised and paid, for the purposes above

[24]

81

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA.

6tated, over thirty·two thousand dollars, and are now in arrears about fourteen
thousand dollars.
Ifor that, they feel justified in callin" upon the Grand and subordinate
Lodge.a o~ the Fraternity, to a.id in re!ie:lng them. No record has been kept
by ~hich it can be. known what exact amount any one sufferer received, be·
!ongmg. to nny particul~r Lodge, of all .that have been provided for. But this
tR c~rt~m ; n? sic~, destitute and suffermg brother has been permitted to suffer
or die .m the:r midst, uncared for .. The noble generosity and disinterestedness with which a few Masons in one of our new cities have contributed so
l~r~~ly to the wants of the brethre!1 ~omi?g in ui.xm them from all parts of the
cmhzed world, has afforded a br1lhant illustration to the world of the excellence and strength of om: principles, and has been most effective in commending our Order to the respect of those who are not of us. Inasmuch as the
effort has produced a common good, and its benefi~ have reached members of
Huch a great number of Lodges, it 11eems but ri"'ht that the existing pecuniary
ob}ig~ti~ns shoi:ld be discharg.ed by a. common ~ort of ;lla&ons.
This circul11;r is ~herefore designed to appeal to the several subordinate Lodges, for a contribution from each, of from five to ten dollars, or more, as their circumstances shall warrant ; to be forwarded through the Secretaries of their reHpective Grand Lodges, to be concentrated in the hands of the Grand Secretary
of the Grand Lodge of New York, in New York City, and by him transmitted
to the Grand Secretary of the Grand Lodge of Oalifurnia or through any more
'
direct channel, as may be most convenient.
Brethren, we do not. think we !'Bk too mueh, nor can we doubt you will give a
prompt response to this appeal m behalf oi these noble few who have become
embarrassed by their devotion to those who have acquired In your Lodges the
right to value upon their brethren on these Western shores. ·
Respectfully and fi-aternally,
,JOHN A. TU'.l'T,
D. STEVENSON,
L. S'fOW'ELL,
1'. A. THOMAS,

The Deputy Grand Master then delivered the following Address : Brethren of the M:. W:. Grand Lodge of California:-

f

In the absence of our respec~d Most Worship~ul Gri;nd Mas~r, th_e plea~­
ing duty devolves on me to preside over your deliberations durmg this semiammal Communication. Permit me to congratulate you on its return, bring·
ing with it, as I hope it does, a happy meeting, trusting that yve ma~, according to our time-honored rules, be linked together by the undymg umon of an
unbroken chain, by which our mystic tie binds us. Meeting as we do to renew the pledges of Brotherly Love, and to inquire for the good and interests
of Free and Accep:ed l\fasonry, we should be thankful to Almighty God for
the watchfulness extended over us to this time. En~aged as we are in the
cause of benevolencP, extending our commiseration where the woes of others
need it, '"e should, by our best exertions, keep the landmarks that guide us to
such action perfectly free from innovation : recollecting that our body being
far above an ephemeral character, we meet for the carrying out of o~jects of
no ordinary nature.
From the best information I have her;n eiiahled to procure, by letters and
otherwise, I am proud in stating that most of the Lodges in the jurisdiction
are in a prosperous condition. As one of the fraternity in this State, . I ~e­
joice in the rapid extension of our time-honored Order throughout the 1ur1s·
diction, and to find that several temples have been erected to its service, and
others projected.
.
In reviewin"' the pro"ress the Order has made since first established a few
months back i':i this St~te, there is indeed much to excite in us gratitude and
pleasure. In the goodness of the ~fost High, our institution has prospered
beyond our most sanguimi expectations, not only in this Far W e~t, but through·
out the civilized world. Although for a long period assailed m other States
by persecution, it has gloriously triumphed, and silently, though .surely, have
we witnessed its pro<>ress; bringin.i:r in its train, wherever established, peace
to the hearts of its fullowers, carrying love to. the familic~ of its m.embers,
suppressing vice, pro?ucing peace ~ud good will, and tend1~g espec.ially, by
its influence, to alleviate the suffermgs of hundreds whof'w1thout friends or
means, found on their arrival he.r~ those w~o, although stranger~, we:~ yet
brothers - brothers ready and w11lmg to assist to the utmost of their ab1hties,
to their necessities incident to coming to a strange and far-off country- far
from home, overcome by appalling sickness 11;nd disease, engendered an~
consequent on tedious travel, and beggared by its consequences. The glon•
ous and glad work of charity thus bei.,run, is still on its onward march, .and
will triuil}ph, so long as the duties incul~ate.d by us are .adh.ere? to ; .and htt}e
fear need be entertained hut that the prmc1ples of our mst1tution will remam
pure and unsullied. so lono- as we abide by its peculiar discipline ; and the
hopes that such will alway~ be so should animate us to persevere in t~e path.
to which it leads and instructs. Let us trust that by the demonstrat10ns of
Masonry, every member thereof, under ap1,>ropriate appliances, ';118.Y hre.ak
through every moral bondage and rise super10r from the degradations which
unfortunately assail and surround the human family.
Communications from several sister Grand Lodges have been received since
our first organization, and we must all feel that the receipt of these documents evinces much true courtesy on the part of the distant fraternity. From
them we are afforded opportunity, in this isolated country, to obtain much
valuable information on Masonic matters. Among those recently received
stands forth the glad and joyous tidings of the ''Union" in New York. Its
consummation must bring joy to the breasts of all, for it is a re· union of gen·
erous hearts that had long 'been separated, but who now feel pleasure in gath·
ering under one banner. Our Committee on Foreign Correspondence will, I

'l'he Grand Secretary, from the Committee on Foreign Correspondence, presented the following report:-

To the M.·. W:·. Gra11d J...odge qf Oalifomia: The Committee on Fore~gn Correspondence respectfully report, that they
have bestowed such attention upon the matters submitted to them as time
and opportunity would permit, and have examined, though cursorily, the published proceedings of other Grand Lodge8, so far as the same have come to
hand, viz.: those of Maine, Vermont, New Hampshire, Rhode Island New
York, Maryland, District of Columbia, Virginia, North Carolina In'diana
Michigan, Wisconsin, Missouri, Mississippi, Arkansas, and Florida. 'Accident'
'
probably, has prevented the reception of others.
It is with great pleasure and pride that the committee are enabled to ass.ure the Grand Lodge that, from all they can gather from these and other reli~ble sources, at no prev!~us period has the ins~itu~ion of Freemasonry occup1e? a more elevated pos1t1on, or been more active rn the benevolent objects
of 1ts creation, than at the present time. That it is altogether free from internal commotion, they regret to say they can not assert ; nor can they hope
that it will ever prove itself impregnable to wrong influences, so long as the

[13]

102

JOURNAL 01'' PROCEEDINGS.

[1Vovember,

human heart shall retain aught of impurity, or the judgment of men shall
continue fallible. But when one shall consider the vast numbers and the
variety of character and pursuits of its members, he wil1 only ~dmi:e tha~ there
is so little to be said of its difficulties in this respect. In tins umty, this oneness of end and aim, consists its stre'ngth and beauty ; ai:;d this i~ is, that h~s
foiled 11.nd put to flight its enemies and cal_ui;nniators .. W.1th gratitu~e and m
humility, every lover of justice should reJO!Ce at tins, t:rnmph of. rig~t over
wrong, of truth over falsehood. In no State of the Umon has this tnumph
been so conspicuous as in Vermont. But the other day her Grand Lodge
had scarcely more than a name in the history of the Order. A few stout
hearts and steady hands, however, remained upon the deck, and among those
may be named without hazard of invidious distinction, a HAZW~JLL and a 'fucKER who never'forsook the helm until safe in port the storm-tossed bark was
safely moored. Now we see that at her annual Communication for_ the cur:ent
year there were fifteen Lodges under Charter, and three under Dispensation,
represented. Until her verdant mountains shall be no more seen, i_uay Freemasonry continue to cheer and gladden the valleys and the plams of the
State of Vermont.
Tn respect to the formation of this Grand Lodge, at the time and under th?
circumstances of its organization, it seems that a questioi;i, not only of expediency, but af Masonic right, has arisen ; but your ?om:n1ttee are pl~D;sed and
"ratified to find that it has not been made a barrier to our recogmt10n, and
that so far as other Grand Lodo-es have been heard from, all have extended
to it the ricrht-hand of fellowship. The question of the right to organize a
Grand Lodge as this was organized, is not now open for discussion; but were
it so, the committee would say to the objector that they.know of no ferei;iptory law requiring a greater number than three Lodges m .a ~tate o; ferritory, to authorize the formation of a Grand ~odge ; and it 1s admitted that
there is at least one precedent for the formmg of a Grand Lodge b~ t?ree
subordinate Lodges, and that too where there ~ere G;and Lo~ges. w1thm a
few hours' travel. With how much more propriety might Cahforn1a do so,
when thousands of miles intervened between her and a Grand Lodge.
While upon the subject of the Institution in this State, it mar n~t be a.miss
to allude to a paragraph in the address of the Grand Master o Missouri,. at
the opening of the Grand Lodge in that State, in May, 1850. In speakmg
of California, he remarks : "I received a commumcat10n from a Master Mason in that distant land, who informe.'l
me, as a friend, that the ties of Masonry even, appeared to him to ha~e lost much of th.cir
binding force in California; that gold, and the thirst for gold, had drie~ up the fou.nt~ms
of Brotherly Love and Relief in that country. That such is _the case 1s _not astomshmg.
Wherever that raging thirst for Mammon seizes ~ fellow-~e~n.g: here with us, even, the
channels of fraternal feeling become dry; the fmer sens1b1h1Ies of the heart become
diseased, and at length !'ease t? _op~rate on his con<;J~ct, bec~use he has ceased to call
them into action ; and his sens1b1hties and the chanl!es of his nature become dead for
want of being used. In such a bosom no sympathy for Freemasonry, no sympathy for
others' woe or weal can be found."

However true these deduetions may be from the premises, the committee
can assure their M:. W.·. Brother that they did not, do not, apply to the character of the fraternity here ; and whoever his correspondent may be, they
can further assure him, that whatllver may have been. the ~ppearance to t~at
Master Mason it is their opinion, that during the terrible wmter of 1849-'oO,
the few Lodg;s then in Calif?rnia\ a~d the in~ividual me~bers of the fraternity, expended more money.m reheymg.the sick and destitute than was .expended during the same period of time m any other ten States of the Umon.
Such wholesale denunciations of Masons, in a country where one man can
know so little of the character of the mass of the fraternity, should be regarded with suspicion, to say the least.

Among the various subjects of greater or less importance, which have engaged the attention of our sister Grand Lodges during the last two years
may be mentioned the following:'
The difficulties in the Grand Lodge of New York:
The propriety of organizing a Supreme or General Grand Lodge of the
United States:
The encroachment upon the territorial jurisdiction of the Grand Lodge of
New York by the Grand Lodge of Hamburg:
The prerogatives of a Grand Master in and out of a Grand Lodge:
.Whether Master Masons, not members of the Grand Lodge, should be perrmtted to be present during the transaction of certain business therein:
Whether it is competent for a Master of a Lodge, or even for the Lodge itself,
to demand of a member his reasons for voting against an applicant for de"rees or for membership ·
,_, Wl~ether the ballot sho~ld be taken prior to the conferring of each degree:
Concerning the phy~ical disqualifications of candidates for the honors of
Freemasonry :
·
And concerning the education of youth, and charitable institutions.
The Grand Lodge of New York has been particularly unfortunate in her
1lomestic relations at more than one period of her history. Prior to 1827

State, each sty_ling itself the_ G_ran? Lodge of New York, and each denying to
the 0th.er the right to tha~ d1stmct10n. In .that year, hapl?ily, the difficulty waA
rcconc1led, and both bodies were harmomously blended mto one. Ten years
elapsed : and then, for causes, or supposed causes, not necesmry now to consider, or even to name, a body styling itself St. John's Grand Lodge was
organized, which obtained little favor either at home or abroad. Pending
this diffic~lty a sc!1ism af;iain occurre~ in the Grand. Lo.dge proper of New
Yo~k, winch conb~ues. violently to a~1tate the fratermty m that State, and to
cxe1te much attent10n m other Grand Lodges of the United States. In the
meantime, however, a reconciliation has been brought about through the friend]~ intervention of neighb?ring Grand L~dgcs, whereby the so-called St. John's
'.'rand Lodge h~s been disbanded, and its officer~ an~ subordina~e Lodges,
and the membc1s of those Lodges, have been received mto harmomous fellowshi~ with the Grand Lodge a?d the Fraternity. This was certainly a consummat10u most devoutly to be wished : and the only difficulty with vour committee
is t.o comprehend how those officers who were expelled Masons-; those Lodges
wlnch ~ot only_t~c Grand Lod(l'e of New York, but nearly every other Grand

can L~ held as L?uges. and M.as:ms duly constituted and in good and regulai'.

The pn~sent diflicult.r in New York, as the committee understand it may be
thns briefly s.tated ; - Previous to 1848, the Past Masters of Lodges had been
members of the (~rand Lodge, and, as Huch, were each entitled to a vote upon
all que~tions coming before that body. At the June Communication of that
ypar, " proposition was made, and received the affirmative vote of the Grand
Lo<l,~·e, .so 1.t~ arnrnd the Constitution as that t~1e perwns who last had passed
tlw ehan· of each Lodge only, should have a right to·vote, while all others were
111Prely to have scats as lwn01w·y members.· Upon the subject of amendments
their Com.titutinn has this regnlation : '

[15]

104

JOURNAL OF PROCEEDINGS.

[November,

"No amendment to this Constitution shall be made, or have any effect, until the same
shall have received the affirmative vote of the Grand Lodge at two successive June Com·
munications; unless, in addition lo the affirmative vote of the Grand Lodge at one June
Communication it shall have received the affirmative vote of a majority of the Lodges
within this jurisdiction. If such proposed amendment shall receive the affirmative vote of
the Grand Lodge, at one June Communication, the same shall be appended to the published proceedings at the end, under the caption, ' Proposed amendments to the Constitu·
tion,' and sent to each Lod~e within this jurisdiction, in order that the Lodges may, if
they think proper, instruct their representatives thereon ; and the action of the Grand Lodge
in relation thereto, shall also appear in the appropriate place in the proceedings."

It will be seen the proposition received the affirmative vote of the Grand
Lodge in June, 1848. It would also seem that the proposed amendment had
been published according to the provisions of the Constitution. At the June
Communication in 1849, a.t the opening of the Grand Lodge, the Grand Secretary stated that the proposition had received the affir)lljl,tive vote of a majority
of the Lodges under that jurisdiction; and thereupon the Grand Master announced that the Constitution was accordingly amended.
A scene of uproar and confusion followed this announcement, most disgraceful to those who created it1 and most painful to contemplate. The acting
Junior Grand W ardet;,i as it is said, declared the Graud Lodge dissolved ! and
thereupon the Senior Urand Warden was called to preside in the assembly
there congregated ; when uuder his assumed authority, they proceeded to elect
officers for the Grand Lodge. Subsequently an election was held under the
direction of the Grand Master. with the aid of such other Grand Officers and
Lodges as adhered to him ; a.nd thus the two bodies, under their respective
officers, claim ea.ch to be the Grand Lodge of N1::w York. The proceedings of
both these bodies, so far as the same have been published, have been received.
They are voluminous, and in some respects contradictory. The subject has
been very fully as well as ably discuased in other Grand Lodges ; and as your
committee have had but little time to devote to the examination of those argu·
ments, they must content themselves wiih such views of their own as ma.y
seem to them sufficient to enable this Grand Lodge to act with intelligence in
the matter, so far as it may be necessary to take action.
At the settlement of the difficulties in 1827, there were certain terms agreed
upon bE>tween the parties in difference, which, in 1844, were denominated a
"Compact," a.nd declared to be a" fundamental law of the Grand Lodge which
never shall. pe disturbed." Among the provisions of this c0mpact are the following: "That there ought to be one Grand Lodge in the State of New York, and tbatit oug·bt
to be held in the City of New York; that the records and archives of the Grand Lodge be·
iug in the Citv of New York, the Grand 8ecrctarx and Grand Treasurer shall be;chosen
from that city; that the Grand Master or lleputy Grand Master shall be chosen, one from
the City of New York, the other from the country, and the two Wardens from some other
part of the State than the City of New York ; that the permanent fund be managed by
tive trustees; that the number of Lodges which one Past Master may represent shall not
exceed three; that Past Masters shall not be represented by proxy; and that representatives be paid as heretofore," &c.

It also appears that, from the time of the organization of the Grand Lodge
as a Provincial Grand Lodge under the Grand Lodge of England, Past Masters were members, with a right to vote in Grand Lodge, as before observed.
ThiR right in the Past Masters was recognized by the ''compact." And now the
fir.st question is, was it competent for the Grand Lodge, &ccording to the rnode
pointed out in the Constitution, to change the Col\stitution so as to limit, in
any respect, the acknowledged right of the Pa.st Masters prior to the arrangement of 1827 ? Secondly, if so, is there any thing in that compact by which
that power was taken away?
The seceders contend that the right of the Past Masters is au inherent right,
the exercise of which could in no respect be curtailed or cont.rolled ; that it is

It will be borne in mind that the Grand Lodge
was in session, and that the Grand Master was in his appropriate place. No
act had been done under the amendment. Whether regular or irregular,
constitutional or unconstitution~lJ as yet nothing had occurred save that single declaration of the Grand Master. As yet that body was tlu; Grand
Lodge, and the Gr!!.nd Officers were in power under the Constitution as it was
a year before. Under all these circumstances, your committee are of opinion that the act of the Wardens, and those who took part with them, was an
act of the grossest insubordination, and unprecedented in the Masonic history
of modern times. Duty, as sentinels at their posts in this Grand Lodge,
painful as is the task, constrains your committee to speak out upon this subject. In doing this they do not forget the fact, that they are speaking of the
ads of those with whom they have taken sweet counsel in days past; and
they now implore those erring brethren to return upon their steps, and beseech
them by their manhood, by their Masonic ties, and by all that men and Masons
hold dear, not to persist in this great error; for, having organized their body
in wrong, they can never bring it into the right way. 'l'he day will come
when they or their successors will pause and reflect ; and the breach, broad
as it may seem to be now, will assuredly be healed, and the sooner it is done
the better it will be for them, and th.e Fraternity everywhere.
Another circumstance has occurred within the territorial jurisdiction of the
Grand Lodge of New York, deserving the attention of this, as also of every
other Grand Lodge in the United States. Upon application from Pythagwas
Lodge, composed of Germans, in the City.of New York, to the Graud Lodge
of Hamburg, Germany, for a Charter to enable it to conduct its business un·
der that jurisdiction, the Senior Grand Master of that body applied to the
Grand Master of New York, for permission to gratify the prayer of the petitioners. Without waiting for a definite reply, a Charter was forwarded by
the Grand Lodge of Hamburg to Pythagoras Lodge, which was duly announced by the Senior Grand .Master to \he Grand Master of New York. It
is hardly necessary to say that New York resists this er1croachment upon her
rights with becoming di11:nity, and with foll determination to protect herself
from a.II such usurpations. We hope and believe that the Grand Lodge of
Hamburg will see the impropriety of the step, and that she will recall the
Charter. If one of the Grand Lodges of a 8tate in our own Union may not
so trespass upon the rights of that of another, much less can we permit one
of another country to set up her authority among us. '!'here needs no argument to show that such a step can not for a moment be tolerated.
The propriety of organizing a Supreme or General Grand Lodge for the
United States, still continues to engage the attention of the several Grand
Lodges. As heretofore, the matter has its warm supporters and determined
opposers. 'fhis question has been so long under consideration, and so often
and so folly discussed, that JOUr committee deem it not worth while to repeat
the arguments, pro and con, nor attempt new ones. The opinion of one of
the committee, at least, is weU known throughout the States ; and although
that opinion remains unchanged, he does not desire again to thrust his reasons in support of it upon his brethren. One great dilli<Julty seems to be the
want of a\Jility in those Grand Lodges, favorable to the measure, to agree
upon the provisions of a Constitution. It is manifest that a compromise
must accomplish this if it ever is accomplished. This might be done by each
Grand Lodge forming a Constitution, such as would suit itself; and then, by
putting it into the hands of delegates, to be depended upon for their skill an.d
iliscretion, who should meet others of like character in genera.I convention,
with authority to compare and alter, a Constitution might be formed acceptable to all.

JOURNAL OF PROCEEDINGS.

[November,

A communication from the Master and Wardens of a Lodge at Panama,
has been placed in the hands of your committee, asking the attention of the
Masonic world to the peculiar condition of that place, and to the utility, and in
fact, the necessity, of buildin"' up there a" Charity Fund, with which to admin·
ister comfort to the sick and distressed, or to bury the dead," of such wayfaring
brethren as may there need such relief. Your committee can well comprehend
that the kind office of the " Good Samaritan" is frequently required there,
more frequently perhaps, at this period, than at any other point in the civilii:ed world ; and would recommend that the Grand Lodge take such action in
the matter as its ability will allow.
The committee do not claim to have even alluded to all the matters of interest which have been recently brought before other Grand Lodges, or to have
discussed those mentioned with the care, or so elaborately, as they would have
been glad to do, and as the subjects deserve. But time has not permitted
them to do more, and they close their report by submitting the following resolutions for the consideration of the Grand Lodge : 1. &solved, That this Grand Lodge recognizes the body of which the M.·. W.•. WM.
H. MILNoR is the presiding officer, as the Grand Lodge of New York, and that Masonic
correspondence shall be held with no other bodv in that State as such.
2. Resolued, That this Grand J,odge can give no countenance or support to the pretension of the Grand Lodge of Hamburg, or any other foreign Grand Lodge, to a right
to grant Charters for Lodges in any of tl1e territory of the United States of America; and that the granting of such to Pythagoras l,odge, in the City of New York, is a
violation of the e.stablislied and well-known usages of Masonry.
3. Resohied, That, from immemorial nsage, the Grand Master of a Grand Lodge has
power to assemble Masons and confer the degrees upon a candidate at pleasure.
4. &solved, That there is no appeal from the decision of the Grand Master in any
matter coming before him for his decision in Grand Lodge, and that the same rule obtains with tlie Master of a snbordinate Lodge over which he presides, or from that of any
other officer while presiding.
5. Resolved, That no member of a Lodge can be required to divulge his vote on balloting for a candidate for the degrees in Masonry, or for membership, nor to assign reasons for his vote, if known.
6. Resolved, That a separate ballot should in all cases be taken prior to the conferring
of each degree, and that one negative defeats the candidate; provided, that when a single negative appears the presiding officer may allow a second ballot to be forthwith
taken, with a view of correcting a mistake and pratided further, that it; upon the stale
of the vote being declared, a member shal announce to the Lodge that he has voted
contrary to bis intention by accident, the ballot may be retaken for correction.
7. Resolved, That the degrees in Masonry should not be conferred upon any one whose
physical deformity is such that he cannot acquire a knowledge and impart to others the
secrels of the Craft, according to the rules of the Order.
8. Resolved, That a committee of three be appointed, whose duty it shall be to cousider and report at: the next meeting of this Grand Lodge, the ways and means of estab·
Jishing a permanent fund for the promotion of education and charitable objects.

.
1

All of which is respectfully submitted.
LEVI STOWELL,
BENJ. D. Hn.M,
CRAs. GILMAN,
J. D. S'l'EYENSON,

Bro. J. NoAH, from the Committee on By-J,aws of J,odges, presented the following report: To the N.·. W. ·. Grand Lodge of California : Your committee, to whom was referred so much of the Address of the R.·.
W.-. Deputy Grand ::\:laster as refers to conferring degrees, respectfully re·
port that they have given the subject due considerat:on, and fully concur
with the Deputy Grand Master, that the time adopted between conferring the
several degrees is too short for the candidate to be proficient in the work;
and they therefore recommend that the application for the first degree lie
over not less than four weeks, and that not less than two weeks intervene between the conferring the subsequent degrees, unless the candidate shall "show,
upon an examination in open Lodge, that he is fully qualified in his work to
advance to the next degree.
Your committee, to whom was also referred so much of the Deputy Grand
Master's Address as relates to the uniformity of the By-Laws of sub01·dinate
Lod"es, beg leave further to report that they fully concur with the Deputy
Gra~<l Master, "that a uniformity of By-Laws is very desirable,'' but are of
opinion that the Grand Lodge has no authority to make By-Laws for the sub·
ordinate Lodges, and that it would be an improper interference with their
rights. The only right the Grand Lodge has reserved to herself, is that of
restraining them in their By-Laws from encroaching npon the ancient Con·
stitutions, and the Constitution of the Grand Lodge.
All of which is respectfully submitted.
JoEJ, NoAH,

J. S. Iawm,
J.M. KooN,
J. D. CaEIGB,

The following amendments to the Constitution were presented : We propose to amend Sec. 3, of Art. IX., by :fixing the price of diplomas at
three dollars - one dollar for the Grand Secretary : To amend Sec. 4, of
Art. IX., by striking out the words " Grand Lodge,'' in the last line, and inserting" Grand Secretary:" To amend Sec. I, of Art. X., so as to allow Lodges
at their discretion to elect their officers semi-annually, at the meetings next
preceding the anniversaries of St. John the :Uaptist and Evangelist: 'fo
amend Art. III. so as to have the regular meetings of the Grand Lodge annual, instead of semi-annual: To amend Sec. 6, of Art. X., so as to require petitions to lay over four weeks for consideration: and to amend Art. XIII., so
that it shall read as follows: "Any proposed amendment or alteration to the
Constitution may be presented to the Grand Lodge, at a regular Communieation, by the representatives of three subordinate Lodges, and, if sanctioned by a majority of the members present, shall be published with the
minutes, as a subject for future action; and, upon its adoption at the next'
regular Communication of the Grand Lodge, by two thirds of the votes given
thereon, the same shall become a part of this Constitution."

2b the M:. W:. Graiid Lodge of California: Your committee regrets that time is not allowed to notice the subject as
he would desire. He appreciates the position of La Fayette and Willamette
Lodges, and can not but approve the course pursued in their associating with
the Grand Lodge of Oregon, as, in this, he feels assured that they were
prompted by a rule regarded as Masonic. The jurisdiction of a Grand Lodge
is usually conceded to be the boundary of the 8tate or Territory where such
Grand Lodge is loeated, but extending to other States or Territories where
no Grand Lodge exists ; and now that those Lodges are not under our imme·
diate jurisdiction, we should none the less regard them with fraternal interest, and as members of one great Masonic family. Their prompt response
to the call made by this Grand Lodge, to subordinate as well as Grand
Lodges, is a sufficient guarantee that they possess the true Masonic qualities,
and in that respect they have set us an example worthy of imitation.
All of which is respectfully submitted.

SEC. 4. The Grand Secretary, Grand 'l'reasurer, and Grand Tyler, shall receive such compensation for their services as the Grand Lodge may, from time
to time, determine.
SEC. 5. It shall be the duty of the Grand Secretary to pay over to the Grand
Treasurer all moneys in his hands belonging to the Grand Lodge, at each regular
Communication, and quarterly, during the recess thereof; and to render an aceount of the same at each semi-annual meeting of the Grand Lodge.
Sim. G. The Grand Treasurer shall receive. all moneJS from the Grand Secretary, belonging to the Grand Lodge, keep a correct record thereof, and, at
each semi-annual Communication of the Grand Lodge, shall render an account
of all his reeeipts and disbursements; but no disbursement shall be made by
him without an order from the Grand Lodge, signed by the presiding officer
and the Grand Secretary, excepting as hereinafter provided.
SEC. 7. The Grand Secretary and Grand Treasurer, prior to their installation, shall execute respectively, to the Grand Master, or, in his absence,
to the Deputy Grand Master, for the time being, to the use of the Grand Lodge,
a bond, with such 5ecurity, und for such sum, as the Grand Master, or, in his
absence, the Deputy Grand Mai;ter, may approve, conditioned for the faithful
discharge of their respective duties.
SEC. 8. All other Grand Officers receiving funds belonging to the Grand
Lodge, shall immediately transmit the same to the Orand Secretary.

SECTION. 1. The Grand Lodge may grant Charter~ for ucw Lodges, to be

Dispensation or otherwise, except upon the recommendation of the Lodge
nearest or most convenient to the place of the proposed new Lodge : .And pi·ovidcd .fill'tlwr, that in case there are two or more Lodges in the same town or

tion of either of them shall be deemed sufficient.
SEC. 2. '.L'hc Grand Lodge shall have full and complete appellate and corrective powers iu all cuses relative to the fraternity within its jurisdiction; and
it shall have power to assess upon its subordinate Lodges such contrilrntions1 from timf' to time, aij the good of the Order may require.
AHTICI,E IX.
iu:n:1't:JoJ.
SECTION. 1. The fee for instituting a subordinate Lodge shall be one hundred and twenty-five dollars, seventy-five dollars of which shall he paid on
the issuing of a Dispensation, und fifty dollars when the Charter is gra11ted ;
but upon the issuing of a Charter, in a case where 110 previous Dispensation
shall have been granted, the whole amount of one hundred and twenty.five
dollars shall be required. Aml every Lodge, upon receipt of its Charter,
shall pay to the Grand Secretary the sum of twenty-five dollars for engrossing
the,same, and allixing thereto the seal of the Grand Lodge.
SEc. 2. Each Lodge shall pay into the treasury of the Grand Lodge the
sum of one dollar fur every degree conferred upon candidates, the sum of one
dollar for every adjoining member, and the sum of one dollar, semi·annual·
ly, for ench contributing member.

SEO. 3. There shall be paid into the hands of the Grand Secretary, for the
use of the Grand Lodge, five dollars, by the applicant, for every Diploma
"'ranted to a member of a subordinate Lodge ; but no Diploma shall issue ex·
~ept upon the presentation of a certifiee.te from tbe Secretary of the Lodge of
which the applicant is a member, of his good and regular standing therein.
Such Diploma, when issued, shall be signed by the Grand Mastel', or Deputy
Grand Master, and countersigned by the Grand Secretary, who shall also affix
thereto the Seal of the Grand Lodge.
SEC. 4. For all and every copy of any portion of the proceedings of the
Grnnd Lodge, or of any document in his possession proper to be published,
which the Grand Secretary shall be required to make, he shall demand and
receive, for the use of the Grand Lodge, fifty cents for every one hundred
words.
ARTICLE X.
SUBORDINATE I.ODGES.

SEO'.l'!ON. 1. Every subordinate Lodge shall elect its officers annually, by
ballot, by a majority of the votes of the members present, at the stated meeting
next preceding the anniversary of St. John the Evangelist, (except in cases
hereinafter provided,) who shall be installed on the evening of their election,
or as soon thereafter as practicable, and shall retain their respective offices
until their successors in office shall be t'lected and duly installed.
SEc. 2. Each subordinate Lodge shall have the right to adopt By-Laws
for its own government, which shall be submitted for the approval of the
Grand Lodge at its regular meeting next ensuing their adoption ; and if ap·
proved, no amendment thereto shall be valid until it shall have received the
sanction of the Grand Lodge.
SEc. 3. Each subordinate Lodge shall assemble for work at least once in
every calendar month ; and if any Lodge shall fail to meet for six successive
months, it shall forfeit its Charter.
SEc. 4. All the proceedings, ballotings, and business of the Lodge, except
that of eonferring the subordinate degrees, shall be had and done in a Lodge
of Master Masons.
Si;:c. 5. No Lodge shall confer the three degrees of Masonry for a less
sum than seventy·five dollars ; and the fee for each degree, as may be reg·
ulated by the Lodge, shall be invariably paid in advance.
SEC. 6. All applications for initiation or membership shall be made in
writina, at a stated meeting of the Lodge; but shall not be acted upon until
after the expiration of two weeks, and then only at a regular meeting. Nor
shall there be conferred more than one degree upon the same candidate at
any one meeting of the Lodge ; and no Lodge shall confer the degrees upon
more than five candidates at any one meeting.
SEc. 7. It shall be the duty of every Lodge working under a Dispensation,
on an application for an extension. of su~h Dispensation, or for a Charter, to
submit its book of records for the 1nspect1on of the Gmnd Lodge.
SEC. 8. Each Lodge shall be represented in every semi-annual meeting of
the Grand Lodge, when it shall also furnish a correct account of its proceed·
in"'s during the past six months, in a form to be prescribed by the Grand
Lgdge, and make payment of its regular dues; and in case of the non·per·
formance of these duties for the space of twelve months, its Charter may be
declared forfeited.
SEc. 9. It is not competent for any Lodge to expel or suspend a member
from all the rights and privileges of Masonry for the non-payment of dues ;
but any member, who shall have neglected to make payment of his dues for
the space of six months, except in case of protracted illness, or absence from
the State, may be snmmoned to appear before the Lodge to show canse why
