cer, and I must emphatically declare that none but chartered Lodges'can
hold a procession, and then under the limitations and restrictions pre·
scribed. Lodges under Dispensation differ very materially from those char·
tered, - not only in the respect mentioned, but they have no power to
perpetuate themselves. They cannot receive members, neither can they
fill vacancies, should their original numbers be diminished below the con·
stitutional requirements, except by special permission from the competent
authority. The essential difference between them and a chartered Lodge
is, that Charters are granted to the petitioners and their successors, while
Dispensations run to the petitioners only, and terminate at the time specified
on their faces. In the latter case the petitioners alone are known in the
Grand Lodge, for, not having been constituted, they possess no power to ad·
mit members. And furthermore, the petitioners, if members of a chartered
Lodge or Lodges at the time of making application for a Dispensation, do
not forfeit, release, or sever themselves from their rights or obligations therein,
until a Charter is granted to them, and being until then a mere association
under probation, and will of the power that gave it birth, although they must
conform to the prescribed work of a Lodge, are not in the capacity or in
possession of the full rights of a Lodge under Charter. A contrary opinion
prevailing in this jurisciction, impels me to direct your attention to the
subject.
Unintentional as it may have been, petitioners for the mysteries, through
their friends, acting under mistaken notions, have been permitted to withhaw
their petitions prior to a ballot being had thereon. No Lodge, under any cir·
curnstances, should ever tolerate a petition to be withdrawn, after it has been
presented and referred. This must, or should be so well understood that I con·
sider it supererogatory to dilate lenghtily on the subject.
A desire to produce uniformity of work in the jurisdiction has been manifest·
ed from the Chair since the early organization of this Grand Lodge, and it will
be, I am fully satisfied, no small amount of gratification to ourselves and
others, if some adopted system could be devised to accomplish it I am fully
aware that numbers of the Fraternity in the jurisdiction feel the par:i.mount
importance and necessity of having all the labors of the respective :Lodges
therein correspond; but under the present system of Grand Lectures it is
found to be perfectly impracticable, and it is to be sincerly hoped 'you will not
separate without defining and decreeing what shall be the aclmoweledged form
and mode, giving it force and effect by proper legislation, so that our ears
may not be assailed by the remarks, "that this is one man's work, and this
the other's."
Many brothers arrive at the Master's Chair who have never even filled any
office in his own, or any other Lodge. The detriment this invariably occa·
sions must be apparent and known to every brother, independent ofprodnc·
i;ip an"ry feelings on the part or those who consider themselves by service, as
'\'v arde~s, entitled thereto and are overlooked. It must be evident to every
brother, that an office so important as that of Master of a Lodge, should be
filled by one who, at least, is fully competent to dischar!!e the duties appertain·
ing to it, but also versed in Masonic jurisprudence, and be intimately acquaint·
ed with the modus operandi of governing a Lodge. There are at all times
points arising in Maaonic law, which, if not properly understO?d, or improperly interpreted, must tend to sap the progress and prosperity of a Lodge;
and as it is the duty of the 'Worshipful Master to fully carry out and see prop·
crly fulfilled the Constitutions of Masonry, ail well of those of the Grand Lodge
under which he holds his power, as also to decide and settle questions of im·
portanee in jurisprudence, and to see the Ily-T,aws of his own Lodge are not
violated or infringed, it' is natural to expect that the brethren will look to
him as the Pillar of Wisdom, for instruction and information; and little re·

The funds arising therefrom were immediately paid over.
I cannot permit the opportunity to pass without expressing the merited
praise Golden Gate Lodge, U. D., is deserving of for the very high perfection
~he has attained in her work. Although an infant, she exhibits the strength
of a giant; her work is unequll.l!ed and her zeal not surpassed by any of her
elder sisters.
I am pained to know that many of our temples have suffered from the de.
structive elements which have swept over various towns in this State during
the past year, and therefore recommend to your liberal c.onsideration the pro- ·
priety of relieving, if compatible with the state of our finances, the following
Lodges from this year's dues, viz.: Tehama Lodge, No. 3; Tuolumne Lodge,
No. 8; Mountain Shade Lodge, No. 18; and Washington Lodge, No. 20.
The prosperity of the Order in this jurisdiction must be a gratification to
all the Fraternity ; and when we consider that three years since there were
only three chartered Lodges, and two under dispensation, and that at this
date we have nineteen chartered, and fifteen Lodges under dispensation, we
have indeed much to be pr.and of.
The circular for and on behalf of our worthy brother JENNINGS, addressed by me to my brother Grand Masters throughout the Union, immediately
:i,fter the adjournment of our Grand Lodge in May last, has, I find, received
due and fraternal attention; and from information at hand, several have press·
ed the subject on the notice of their subordinate Lodges, and I doubt not
before we assemble again, a considerable amount will be in our possession to
assist the liquidation of the debt due by that true Mason, incidental on the unli_mited, boundless charity and brotherly feeling extended to his suffering and
1hstressed brothers.
'!.'he R.·. W:·. Deputy Grand Master, during my short absence from the jurisdiction, was invested with the care of the Craft. A report of his labors will
doubtless be presented to you.
Before concluding, allow me to solicit that you will decide in what manner
the funds in my possession, amounting to three hundred dollars, are to be
t!isposed of. In such direction as you may order them to be appropriated, I
can only assure yon it will be perfectly satisfactory to me, and receive my cor·
dial cooperation.
BRETHREN: I will not oecupy your time longer, craving your kind indulgence
for what I have taken up, but solicit you to look well to your duties as Masons;
have respect for the discipline and landmarks of the Order, and the rule of
conduct the institution inculcates; live and act in accordance with the principles and obligations taught by them, and during the discussion of the
various important questions that I have directed your attention to, remember
that we are all brethren, bound tog-ether by ties that no other institution pos·
Hesses. 'l'herefore let no contentions arise but that "Noble contention or
mnulation, who can best work and best agree. "
I need, in conclusion, only say, that so far as depends on me, I shall be found
ready and willing to aid all la.wful measures, for the best interests of tho
Order, and hesitate ere I relax any of the feeling I have always entertained
for it, from the time I first beheld its light.
:B. D. HYAM1 Grand Master.

the Grand Lodge of Cal(fornia: By the indulgence of our Supreme Grand Master, we are once more per·
mittcd to meet in this sacred r0treat of friendship and brotherly love. And

[Jl.ay,

while we exchange congratulativns, let us remember our responsibility to
God, our neighbors, and ourselves. Lµ,t us see to it that our deliberations at
this time be conducted in harmony and and brotherly love-let our guiding
star be the advancement of our beloved Order.
I would call the attention of this worshipful body to the subject of Uniformity of Work in the Lodges in this jurisdiction. Masons are thrown ·together in this State from every part of the world, and there are few of them
who work alike; which tends to create confusion and discord among the
Craft. And there is a great diversity, even :i,mong the officers of the Grand
Lodge. Should this desired object be brought about by your legislation at
this Communication, it would tend to allay the schisms that have, or would
naturally arise from a diversity of opinions, and yonng Masons would study
with a view to excel in good work. Before leaving this subject, permit me to
suggest that one Grand Lecturer be appointed by the Grand Lodge, who
shall exhibit a specimen of his work, and if approved by the Grand Lodge1
let him be instructed to visit every Lodge in the fltate, and teach said work.
Let this Grand Lecturer be some well informed brother, who will devote bis
time to the matter; and that his salary be equal to bis duties, let the subordinate Lodges be severally taxed in an amount equal to the salary and ex·
penses of said Lecturer, and that the several Lodges shall pay over to the
Lecturer the amount taxed against them.
I must call your attention to a subject of the utmost importance to Masonry
in this jurisdiction : that of non-affiliated Masons. Let me assure you that
at least two-fifths of all the Masons in this State are brethren who contribute
nothing to the support of the institution ; yet we are constantly called upon
to relieve them iu their distresses. I know that a fund of some four thousand
five hundred dollars has been expended by Tuolurnne Lodge since its organi·
zation, to relieve non-affiliated ltfasons. Hence I would suggest that a tax
be imposed on all non-affiliated Masons, equal to the dues of a Lodge, and
when a sojourning brother pays his tax int0 any Lodge in the jurisdiction,
said Lodge shall give the brother a certificate, which shall be taken as evi·
deuce of his good standing, (so far as documentary evidence can show that
fact,) and enable him to visit any Lodge in this State, and he entitled .to all
the privileges of Masonry. I cannot but believe that a Mason who has so
far lost sight of his duty to the Fraternity, as to visit every Lodge in his
travels, for years, without C'Ontributing his mite for its support, is no~ entitled
to our sympathies, and should not expect to receive that protection and relief
which is due to worthy contributing members.
The subject of educating the children of indigent Masons, demnnds your
attention at this time, if a fnud could be raised for that purpose. I will here
remark, that in the mountain regions Masons have not a house they can .call
their own. Many Lodges, from fire and flood have lost all, and have been
taxed individually beyoml their means; notwithstanding all these casualties,
u needy brother' has· never been sent empty away. Any plan devised by
your Most W orshipfol hody, whereby a fond can be raised for the purpose of
Education, will meet with my hearty support.
I deeply regret that our brethren in New York are still at variance with
each other, but hope that they may listen to the voice of reason, and say with
one accord, "there shall be but one shepherd and one sheep-fold."
My term of office as Deputy Grand Master now expires, and here allow me
to offer my thanks to all those with whom I have had the honor to be associated during the past year, and for their assistance and co<Jperation iu the
duties I have had to perform. In concluding, permit me to assure you, that
whatever be the result of your deliberations at this meeting, you shall have
my cordial support; trusting that your labore may tend to the advancement
of our beloved Order, and that all your discussions may be conducted in the •

may we be called to sit upon the right-band of the great I AM, fit and fully

C. M. RADCLIFF, Deputy Grand Master.

The Grand Secretary, by virtue of a resolution of the Grand Lodge, has
continued the subscriptions for the various Masonic journals, and has purchased
a book·ca.se, writing desk, and other necessary fixtures for his office ; and he
has drawn upon the Grand Treasurer for payment of the same, and other
necessary expenses of the Grand Lodge. He would again bring to the no·
tice of the Grand Lodge the faet that no secure or convenient place has been
procured for a Grand 8ecretary's office, wherein to deposit the records, jewels,
clothino-, and other property of the Grand Lodge.
The tlrand Secretary has not, as yet, received any Grand Lodge diplomas,
bnt is in daily ex:pectation of receiving them. He regrets that he has to re·
port that only five Lodges sent in their semi-annual returns for November
last, as required by the Grand Lodge. 11his, and other like omissions of
prescribed duties, may seem trivial on the part of each Lodge, but a moment's
reflection must surely convince them of ihe serious evils and inconveniences
resulting from the non-performance of these necessary duties. They operate
detrimentally to the interests of the Grand Lodge, prevent an orderly, prompt,
and proper performance of its duties, and render it impossible for the Grand
£ecretary to discharge his duties with a.ny satisfaction to himself, or credit to
his office. He would, therefore, earnestly entreat of his llrethren a reforrna.·
tion in this respect.
'fhe printed proceedings of several sister Grand Lodges, and various other
communications ha.v.e been received, (many of them lately,) and were retained
in the Grand Secretary's office, in consequence of the absence of some of
the Committee on }foreign Correspondence, and the inability of the others to
perform the duties of the committee. A short report, howe~er, will be made,
so far as a partial examination of the document:; and the limited time allotted,
will permit.

The first amendment proposed that Sec. I, of Art. V., be so altered
as to read as follows : SEv. l. Each of the Grand Officers for the time being, and every elective
Past ~rand Officer, if present at a meeting of the GraBd Lodge, shall each
be entitled to one vote. Each Lodge shall be entitled to three votes by its
prnper officers, -or, in their absence, their proper representatives, - and
the Past Masters of' each Lodge collectively to one vote. No member, in his
own right, shall be -entitled to more than one vote.

Bro. J. D. CRBrGH moved that its further consideration be indefinitely postponed; which lUOtion did not prevail, and the amendment
was adopted.

report:To the JI:. W: '. Grand Lodge of California :
The Committee on Foreign Correspondence beg respectfully to report that
the following communications have been i·eceived from various sister Grand
Lodges, viz. : - The printed proceedings of Louisiana, 1852-3 ; Florida,
1852; Arkansas, 1851-2; Georgia, 1851 : Mississippi, 1852; Maryland,

l\Iassachusetts, 1851-2; Wisconsin, 1852; New Hampshire, 1852; Pennsyi'vauia, 1851; District of Columbia, 1853; Canada West, 1851; and several
communications from the Grand Orient of Frirnce.
In eonsequence of the absence of those memJiers of the committee upon
whom it was e~pect.ed t~e. duties for the past year would de1·ol ve 1 the report
must of necessity be brief, and we throw ourselves upon the leniencv of the
Grand Lodge, as they well know the multiplicity of duties that have devolved
upon the ex,officio chairman of the committee, the past vear; and we can
bnt express our thanks to Bros. II. F. WrLLTAllS and A. d. AHELL for their
valuable assistance in preparing this report.
We can with unfeigned pleasure congratulate our brethren upon the continued and unexampled prosperity of onr beloved Order throughout everr
portion of onr vast Hepublic.
With triumphant joy we hail the onwaril
march of the pure and benign principles of Masonry 1 which tend so effectually to elevate and enlighten the hnman understanding, \Yben properly appreciated. Never were her prospects more flattering; redolent with the
brightest hopes, and gathering within her folds the pure in morals, the elevated i11 inrnllect, the Stutesman, the Divine, the high and the low, as one
great baud of wm·kmen, each laboring in his own proper sphere, to advance
the high and holy 1Yis.oion of Jfasonry.
But while these eheering salutations come pouring in upon us from every
quarter, let. us be cautious of a too rapid growth. Your committee cannot
express their viewR upon this point better than by giving a quotation from
the able report of the Committee on Foreign Correspondence of the Grand
Lodge of Georgia, as follows : "While congralulatiug you upon the growth aud ilourishiug condition of Masonry
within ,our borders, your committee depre.cale a too luxuria)lt growth; weeds may be
sprmgmg up, concealed by the golden gram, and when the harvest is gathered evil and
woe mny befall us, and from a <1uar1er least expected - our own midst. We' earnestly
advise our Graud and Deputy Grand .!\fosters to grant Dispensations with great caution·
to he careful to ascertain that a new Lodge is needed in the vicinity for which it is asked'
and that the brethren who asked for the Dispensation arc not only worthy men hut com~
pctcnt to discharge the dut~cs :which they take upoll themsc]ves. A Lodge sh~uld be to
the Graud Lodge what an rnd1V1dual Mason should be to l11s Lodge-not a drone in the
hive, but an. adive. zc.alous mcr:iber-not a.detriment but an ornament. A Lodge
should be ns Jealo11s of its reputation and stand mg as a l\fason should lie of his character and honor. We trust, therefore, that our Grand Officers will use great care and
deliberation, and grant no Dispensations unless imperatively needed."

This caution we would extend to our subordinate Lodges, in recommending petition.a for Dispensations to the Grand and Deputy Grand Masters.
We notice mauy complaints are made of our brethren of New York and
South Carolina, for making Masons of those not residents in their jurisdictions, Your committee also deprecate this practice, and will state a case of
late occurrence, in which we think our New York brethren did not oo as they
would be done by. A candidate (a resident of this city,) for the degrees of
Masonry, was rejected in California Lodge, No. 1, and soon afterwards left

caused onr present pecuniftry embarrnssmeut. But the energy and generosity

who have been among us, can form correet ideas of our position in this respect ; yet it is a pleasing reflection to know that we have thus far been able

and have been enabled to carry out tlic great object of Maaonry-Oliarity to
all rnankind.
Your committee cannot resist the temptation to lay before this Grand Lod"e
a short address delivered by our much esteemed brother, B. B. FRENcn, Gra~d
Master of the District of Co1umbia, upon the occasion of laying the cornerRtone of the new Capitol at w·ashington, D. C., as follows: -

iiberallv, in proportion to its means.
Many donations have been mnde, which han• not yet ~ome to hand. ~!­
though the conditi.on ?four State h~s impr.ov<;? mucb, socially ar.1d. oth<:rw1se,
and thoucrh tbere 1s far less c;iuse for dest1tnt1011 and actual hnlteri.ng among·
us than tfirrnerlv, still, while our population continues to increase so rapidly
as at present, it must be expected (as is certainly ;he ca~e) that many will be
thrown amonrr us sick and deotitute, who need a~sistan<:e ; a1.d this keeps the '
ttumber of applicants for charity as large or larger tlurn in 184\) and ~850.
Hence our Lod"'es are unable to accumulate a eharit.y fou<l, and our pnvate •
resources have "been, and are constantly heavily taxed. None, save thO!le '.

M11 Masonic Brethren:l arise to address you on this occasion, deeply oppressed with the circumstances which
surround me.
Standing, as I do, in the presence of som\'l of the most exalted men of this nation, and
t? be followed, as I am to be, hy oue admitted hy all as cmj>hatically the orator of his
time, and of whom I ean truly say, "He it is, who, coming after me, is preferred before
me. -':"hose shoe's latchet I am not worthy to unloose," you will believe me guiltv of no
affectat10n, when I say 1 feel a diHidcnce, which is to me unusual. Still, as ymu 'Grand
l\Iaster, I have a duty to perform, and J shrink from no duty, under any circumstances.
As has been the custom of our revered Order 011 such occasions, I shall proceed briefly
to address you.
•
I am unable to conceive of a more interesting occasion than this upon which we arc
assembled, on this anniversary of the birth-day of American Freedom. Here we arc the prond dome of our Capitol towering above us, - assembled together from the n~rth
and the south, from the east and the west, to perform a duty, indicative in itself, of the
growth and prosperitv of this mighty nation.
. Ou the eighteenth Jay of September, 1793, was laid by GEORGE WASHINGTON, President of the United States and Grand Master of Masons, (at least on that occasion) the
corner-stone of the magnificent edifice before us. It was, doubtless. supposed that when
completed according to the plan then adopted, it would be of ample dimensions to accommodate all the wants of the people by whom it was to be erected for ages then to
come. Fifty-eight years have elapsed, and, in that comparativelv brief space i11 the
age~ ?f gove~nments, .we arc called upon to assemble here and lay the corner-stone of an
a~dit1onal edifice, wh.1ch shall hereafter to':"er up, resting firmly on the strong foundation
this day planted, addmg b.eau~y and magmtude to the Peopl".'s !muse, and illustrating to
the .world the firm foundation m the People's hearts, of the prmc1ples of freedom and the
rapid growth of those principles on this western rnntinent.
Yes, my brethren, standing here, where, fitly-eight vears ago, \VASIHNGTON stood
olothcd in the same Masonic rPgalia that he then wore; using the identical gavel that h~
11<.ed, we have assisted in laying the foundation of a new Capitol of the United States
tlus day, ?;s Solomon of 01.d laid the foundation. of th!' temple of the living Gou: "Now,
lbere(ore,' says the historian Josephus," the kmg lmd the foundation of the Temple very
deep m the ground, and the materials were strong stones, and such as would resist the
force of time;" and we, following this sublime example, have laid here, deep in the

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA.

265

they have examined said reports, together with the books and vouchers of said
officers, and find that the accounts of the Grand Lodge, up to the present annual Communication, have been correctly kept.
The committee wouJd farther report that the Donation Fund, up to the
present time, amounts to the snm of nine hundred and seventy-five dollars
and eighty cents, ($975 80.) That there is now in the hands of the Grand
Treasurer the sum of six hundred and sixty-nine dollars and eighty-six cents.
($669 86,) leaving the Grand Lodge in debt to the Donation Fund, three hundred and five dollars and ninety four cents, ( $305 94.) This arises from the
fact that but five Lodges paid up their November dues to the Grand Lodge;
and the committee would earnestly but respectfully call the attention of
every member of the Grand Lodge to that part of the Grand Secretary's re·
port which so forcibly points out the difficulty and confusion which ensues
from a neglect of the subordinate Lodges in this particular.
While upon this subject, the committee would respectfully suggest that the
mode of making returns to the Grand Lodge is not at all calculated to facilitate the business of that body, or to simplify its accounts.
Upon the first day of the Annual Communication, at the very time when
the undivided attention of the Grand Secretary should be given to the proceedings of the Grand Lod,ge, there is piled upon his table an immense mass
of returns and accounts sufficient to occupy his attention for several days, and
making it necessary to carry the accounts of the last six months (and when
there arc no November returns the accounts for the year,) over into the accounts of the next year. The committee would therefore recommend that the
present plan be changed, that the returns of the subordinate Lodges may be
made up to a period of two months before the meeting of the Grand Lodge,
and that such Lodges as do not make their returns to the Grand Secretary,
and pay up their duos to the Grand Lodge, be not allowed a vote in that body
unless excused by the Grand Lodg-e. This would give the Grand Secretary
the necessary time to get up the business of the Grand Lodge ; it would enable each year's accounts to be kept by themselves, and would insure the attention of the subordinate Lodges to the matter of paying up their dues.
The committee would also report that i11 their opinion the fees paid to the
Grand Lodge for conforring the degrees, is not in proportion to the fees received by the subordinate Lodges. U ndcr the jurisdiction of the Grand
Lodges generally throughout the Uniou, the subordinate Lodges receive only
fifteen dollars to thirty dollars for conferring the three degrees, out of which
tho Graud Lodge receives from fifty cents to one dollar and fifty cents on each
degree conferred. Whereas, under this jurisdiction, the subordinate Lodges
receive from seventy-five dollars to one hundred and fifty dollars for conferring the three degrees, and our Grand Lodge only receives one dollar for
each degree.
The committee woulcl further report that the Grand Secretary is by no means
properly remunerated for his services, either in the mattnr of salary or perquisites.
During the last year the Grand Secretary hns received, as salary, but six hundred dollars, while the duties of his oltice are such 11s to require nearly the
const~nt a~tentio~ of ?ue .person, or the \1artial att~ntion of several. By an
oversight rn the Const1tut10n, the Grand 8ocretary 1s not allowed to receive a
fee for a Dispens11tion, although it requires as much, if not more labor, than
that of making out a Charter. By an amendment of the Constitution, the fee
for a diploma was reduced from five to three dollars, of which sum the Grand
Secretary receives but one dollar; a fee by no means commensurate with the
duties to be performed, viz.: -that of communicating with the brother, recording his certificate, making out his diploma., and attach in CJ' the seal of the
Grand Lodge.
"
'
Tho committee would therefore recommend that the Grand Lodge (before

2I

[43)

266

[May,

JOURNAL OF PROCEEDINGS.

the election of officers,) fix upon the salary of the Grand Secretary, which
shall be sufficient to enable him to give his entire attention to the duties of his
office. They would also recommend that the Grand Secretary be authorized
to procure an office in a fire-proof building, for the better preservation of the
1.rchives and books of the Grand Lodge.
All of which is respectfully submitted.
vyM. ROBINSON, } Committee.
SAMUEL MARX,

To the M:. W.-. Grand Lodge of California : Your committee to whom was referred the report of the Committee on Finance and Accounts, report the same back, recommending the concurrence of
the Grand Lodge therein, and the adoption of the following resolutions:Resolved, That the sum of twelve hundred dollars be appropriated to Bro.
L. Stowell, as a compensation for his efficient services as Grand Secretary of
the Grand Lodge during the past year.
Resolved, That the salary of the Grand Secretary of the Grand Lodge of
the State of California for the year next ensuing, be twelve hundred dollars.
Resolved, That the Grand Secretary be authorized to procure an office in n.
fire-proof building, for the better preservation of the archiYes of the Grand
Lodge.
Your committee also report the following proposed ameudmen ts to the Constitution as properly signed, and recommend their adoption: We propose to amend Sec. 1, of Art. X., so that it shall read, "Every
subordinate Lodge shall elect its officers annually or semi-annually, (as they
may deem fit,) by ballot, by a majority of the votes of the mAmbers present
e.t the stated meeting next preceding the anniversaries of St. John the Evangelist and St. John the Baptist, who shall be installed on the evening of their
election, or as soon thereafter as practicable, and shall retain their respective offices until their successors in office shall be elected and duly installed."

S UBORDIN A.~rE LODGES
ARTICLE III., Section 1. 'l'he regular Communications of the Grand Lodge
shall be held in Sacramento city, or at such place as the Grand Lodge may
from time to time designate, on the first Tuesday in 1foy in each year; and
the election of Grand Officers shall take place us horeinafter provided.
(Adopted, May, A. L. 5852.)
ARTICLE V., Section 1. Each of the Grand Officel'S for the time being,
and every elective Past Grand Officer, if present at a meeting of the Gralld
Lodge, shall each be entitled to one vote. Each Lodg-c shall be entitled to
three votes by it~ proper officers, or, in their absence, their proper rcpresen·
ta.tives; and the Past Masters of each Lodge collectively, to one vote. No
member, in his own right., shall be entitled to more than one vote. (Adopted,
May, A, L. 5853.)
ARTICLE IX., Section ::. '!'here shn.ll he paid into the hand~ of the Grand
Secretary three dollars, by the applicant, for evHy diploma granted to a member of a subordinate Lodge, (one f1ollar for the Grand Secretary : ) but no
diploma shall issue, exeept upon the presentation of a rcrtilicate from the
Secretary of the Lodge of which the applicant i~ a membe1·, of lds good and
regular standing therein. Such diploma, when issued, sl1all lie signed by the
Grand Master, or Deputy Grand llfoster, rin'l countcrnil.!'ned hy the Graµd
Secretary, who shall affix thereto the seal of the Gram\ Loilge. (Adopted,
May, A. L. 5852.)
ARTICLE IX., Section ,[. l<'or all aud every copy of any portion or the proceedings of the Grand Lodge, or any document in possession of the Oraud
Secretary, which he shall he required to make, he shall demand aud receive
for his use fifty cents for every one humlred wor(k (Adopted, May, A. I..
5852.)
ARTICJ,E X., Section 2. Bach subordinate Lodge shall lrnve the right to
adopt By-Laws for its own government, which shall be submitted for the ap·
proval of the Grand Lodge ; and, if approved, l!O amendment thereto shall be
valid until it shall have received the sanction of the Grand J,odge, Grand
Master, or Deputy Grand Master. (Adoptod, ~fay, A. L. 5853.)
ARTICLE X., Section 5. No Lodge within this State shall confer the three
degrees of Masonry for a less sum than seventy-five dollars ; and no Lodge
under the jurisdiction of this Grand Lodge, and out of the State, shall confer
the three degrees of Masonry for a less sum than thirty-five dollars ; in each
case the fee for t'ach degree, as may be regulated by the Lodge, shu.ll be in·
variably paid in advance. (Adopted, May, A. L. 5853.)
ARTWU XIII., Section L
Any proposed am~ndment or alteration to thi~
Constitution may lie presented to the Grand Lodge at a regular Communica·
tion, by the representatives of tliree subordinate Lodges ; and, if sanctioned by
a majority of the members present, shall be published with the minutes, as a
subject for future action, and upon its adoption at the next regular Communication of the Grand Lodge, by two thirds of tlrn votes giveu thereon, the
same shall become a part of this Constitution. (Adopted, May, A. L. fl852.)

IlRETHREN ; By the indulgence of our Supreme Grand Alaster we arc once
more permitted to meet in our annual Communication.
Permit me to express the pleasure with which I again meet the protectors of
our beloved Order within these sacred walls, and whilst we exchange our friendly
congratulations, let me ask yon to unite with me in grateful thanks to Him
who rules the destinies of man, for his protection during our separation, and
to ask His blessing on our present convocation, and that he may so govern us
that all our labors may be conducted in harmony and brotherly love.
I have the gratification to announce to your 'Vorshipful body the continued
prosperity of our beloved Order in this State, and the rapid extension both of
its members and influence. lliasomy is at last appreciated as a moral
science, whose system, when developer! hy tongues and pens equal to the
task, must enlighten those who wander in darlrncss; and let ours be the glo·
rious competition, or rather emulation, of who shall best and most contribute
to effect the noble objects for which onr institution was intended, aud wliich it
is undoubtedly it.s destiny sooner or later to accomplish.
The reports which I have received relative to the Lodges in our jurisdiction,
represent them in a prosperous condition. At no previous period in the Masonic history of this State was there ever folt such an i11fluenco as that which
is now stimulating the brethren within her limits. This we may mainly attribute to the influence of the liberal character of our regulations, to the confidence with which all have been animated by t.hc feeling of an equal share
and voice in the government of their own nffairs, and the conviction that the
Order in this State haH not only mttintaincd its self'.rcspcct, !mt has also
~aincd the esteem ttll(l consideration of our brethren abroad.

[ am enthusiastic in the en.use, I dread thu result

~our committee regard the practice of certain Lodges, in the matter of
their wor~ in the E. A. and F. C. degrees, as referred to in the said address,
as clearly irregular, and respectfully su"gest that the same be referred to the
Committee on Uniformity of Work, to ::'eport thereon at this Communication.
Your committee are of opinion that Lodges working under Dispensation
have as full and undoubted authority for admittin" brethren to membership
"'
as chartered Lodges.
In reference to that portion of said report touching the suspension by
Lodges of members for non-payment of dues, we cannot conceive a practice
more unjust to a brother, or more inexcusable in a .Lod,,e in view of the
"' '
plain sense of Art. X., Section \I, of the Constitution.
, . As nothing can contribute in a higher degree to the preservation and din«
mty of the Cm.ft than perfect uniformity in the manner of its work the exc~l­
lent suggestions of the Grand Master in this behalf are earnestly c~mmendecl
lo the consideration of the Committee on Uniformity of Work.
'l1he deplorable schism which hall existed for some time amonO" the Masonic
Br?therhood in New York, disfiguring the otherwise harmoniius spectacle
whie~ Masonry presents throughout the world, is seriously to be lamented ;
tL1:d !t ~nrely become~ t~is Grand I;odge to exert all the means legitimately
withm its power, t.o atd lll re-eollectm" the broke.n fra11ments and restoring
th~ temple of our brethren in that gr~at State to its former' granclenr. To
this end the salutary suggestions of the Grand Master strong1y recommend
them.selve~ to the attention of this Grand I;odge, and especially to the earnest
cons1derahon of the Committee on Foreign Correspondence, to which the
s!l.me properly belongs.
·
The education of ~he children of Masons has ever heen a darling subject
nmong our brethren m other States, and should engage the affections of every
good Mason. Your committee therefore recommend the creation of a Com·
mittee on Education, with instructions to report, t.t an early day a scheme or
plan the best calculated, in their judgment, to carry ont the rec~mmendations
of the Grand ]\faster in this behalf.
The necessity of fixing upon some point for the permanent seat of this
Grand L?dge, is obvious; and for this purpose your committee would suggest
the apporntment of a special committee, consisting of three Past Masters, to
report the most smtable place for the fntnre permanent sittings of this Grand
Body.

'fhe following resolutions, presented by the Committee on Masonic
Jurisprudence with their report this morning, were taken from the
table:Resolved, That the practic<l of dueling i.~ repugnant to tho prinoiples of
Freemasonry, and in all cases where two brethren resort to this mode of
settling their disputes, it becomes the duty of the Lodge or Lodges of which
they are members, or under whose jurisdiction they may be, forthwith to
expel them from all the right~ and privileges of Masonry, subject to the con·
firmation of the Grand Lodge; and no Brother who may fall in a dnel shall
be buried with Masonic honors.
Resolved, That the Grand Secretary is hereby constituted Grand Librarian,
and directed to take charge of the Grand Lodge Library, subject to such regulations a~ the Grand Lodge shall hereafter prescribe.
Resolved, 'rhat it is the sense of this Grand Lodge, that the future estab·
lishment of subordinate Lodges should be restricted to cases of inanifest pro·
priet,l/ aud necessity.

JOURNAL 0!' PROCEEDINGS,

with many of the leading members of the Fraternity, was produced by this
election, which, at this juncture of Masonic affairs in New York, was calculated to result in much harm. A convention of thirty-two Lodges recently
assembled at Rochester to consider the propriety of dividing the State into
two Masonic jurisdictions, and nearly unanimously declared it. expedient to
do so. This question involves a matter of vital importance to all Grand
Lodges, and is replete with disastrous consequences. We cannot for a moment entertain the idea of there being more than one Grand Lodge in the
same State or Territory; for, once concede the right or principle of division,
and there is no limit to the number of Grand Lollges in any State except as
the number three may be contained in the whole number of subordinate
Lodges. This Grand Lodge holds t!1e <kctrine that but one Grand Lodge
ce.n exercise Masonic jurisdiction over the same territory at a time; and we
believe this is the general, if not the unanimous opinion of the Grand Lodges
of the United States. The Grand Lodge of California 1·ecognizes as the legal
Grand Lodge of New York, that over which the Hon. Hcuben H. Walworth
presides as Grand J\faster, and of which Jas. M. Austiu is Grand. Secretary.
In' addition to the regular Grand Lodge of New York, there are also the
fo!Iowing illegal and self-constituted bodies, viz. : the bodv of which Mordecai
Myers is Grand Master, and James Herring, Grand Secretary; the St. John's
Grand .Lodge, of which Richard Thum is Grand Master, and Chas. W. Willetts, Grand Secretary ; the Scottish Rite Lodges of II. C. Atwood, and the
Lodges working under the Grand Lodge of Hamburg. This Grand Lodge
has already remonstrated against the intrusion of the last named, in her pretended jurisdiction within the territory of another Graul! Lodge, and does now
unequivocally place its seal of condemnation upon this most palpable wrong.
The Grand Lodge of Louisiana has still some Masonic difficulties, arising
out of the granting of charters to Symbolic 1.odges by a body styling itself a
Supreme Council of the 33d Degree. A sketch of this matter, also, was
given in a report to this Grand Lodge two years ago, and the disapprobation
of the Grand Lodge was then recorded.
Your committee would allude to I\ snhject heretofi:n«" deemed unworthy of
notice, and which would now he passed in silence, had there not been some
little agitation, in some quarters of our jurisdiction relative to the matter,
viz.: Negro Masonry. 'Ve will not discnss the subject, but refer the brethren to the Freemasons' Magazine, for an account of these pretended J,odges.
It is there stated that ''there is not a bwfully authorized or acknowledged
negro Lodge in tbe United States, nor are we aware that there is a negro in
the country who could rightfully claim, or properly be admitted into any
lawfol Masonic Lodge.'' We have no satisfactory eviden<'e that it i~ recognized by any Grand Lodge in the United States, and none can be held ac.
countable for its existence. 'Ve must therefore hold all such to be spurious,
and the remedy is not with us.
Your committee regard it as important that this Grand Lodge express an
opinion upon the true character and position of the Past :Master's degree, as
there has been some considerable clashing of opinions among the brethren
and Lodges in this jurisdiction in regard to it. We quote Bro. A. G.
MACKEY's remarks upon the subject, as affording a better version of it than
we could give of our own. He says "There is no doubt, if we carefully examiue the history of Masonry in this country and
in E11gla11d, that the degree of Past Master was originallv conferred by Symbolic Lodges
as an honorarium, or reward bestowed upon tl1osc brethren who had been found worthy
to occupy the oriental chair. In so far it was only a degree of office, and could be obtained only from the Lodge in which the office hat! been conferred, At a later period it
was deemed an essential prerequisite lo exaltatiou in the degree of Royal Arch, and was
for that purpose conferred on candidates for that positiou, while the Royal Arch degree
wa.s under the control of the Symbolic Lodges, hut stiil only conferred by the Pa.st Mas-

'l'he receipt of the money and order has been acknowledged to Bros.

above hooks, we also ordered a set of the Freemason's Magazine, of'Bro. C. \V.
Moore of Boston, which has been received, consisting of eleven volumes,
neatly bound, at the cost of $55.
Notwithstanding the Lodges have been repeatedly solicited to be prompt in
the performance of their duties, still most of them fall far short of the requirements of the Grand Lodge. Ten Lodges only have sent in their semi-annual·
returns, viz.: No. 1, No. 3, No. 5, No. 18, No. HJ, No. 24, No. 28, No. 29,
No. 30, No. 31; and about the same number have sent to the Grand Secre·
retary's office, lists of t],e officers elected for the present ~Iasonic year, though
each Lodge has been requested to do so. Hence the Gmud Secretary has been
compelled to address his commnnieations to the old officers, antl doubtless
many have never been received bv the Lod,,.es. In some cases where the officers ·had gone to the Atlantic States, the letters were forwarded to them, and
after several months were returned and received bv the Lod,,.es. These and
other omissions of duties by the subordinate Lodg.:s, are the" causes of many
vexations delays and much loss of t.ime, and extra labor on the part of the of~
ficcrs of tlie Grand Lodge; and it. must be evident to those who have attended
the Gro.nd Lodge from time to time, that among the many subjects that come
before the (hand Lodge for adjustment, information and regulation, bv far the
greater portion arise for want of information of what the Grand Lodge has
done, ignorance of the edicts and resolutions passed, and of the changes made
in the Constitution. The officers, at least, of each Lodge should be familiar
with the proceedings of the Grand Lodge ; and to he so, requires that they
read them frequently and carefully, that the hnsiness of the Lodge may be
conducted in a proper and ilfasonic form.
The Grand Secretary would rcspectfnlly sugge~t the propriety of making
the Lodge returns mmual instc>id of semi-annual, believin"' that the purposes
of the Grand Lodge will he equally well serv~d, and the I~odges be saved the
labor. or the offence of omitting their dntv.

Bro. H. G. Pr,ATT, from the special committee on the permanent
location of the Grand Lodge, presented the following report : -

The Grand Lodge was then called o:ff until 9 o'clock on Monday
morning next.

'.lb the M. ·. W. ·. Grand Lodge of California : The committee to whom was referred the subject of permanently locating
the Grand Lodge, beg leave to report that it has received the serious attention and consideration of your committee, who deem the permanent location
of this Grand Lodge a matter of great importance ; and in the selection of a
place, they have been governed by the advantages that would accrue to the
fraternity generally, by the facilities for the transaction of business, the convenience of members, and the opportunities for improvement in Masonic
knowledge, and collecting in the vicinity of the Grand Lodge all that is necessary and useful for the encouragement and protection of our time.-honored
institution. They submit the following resolution : &solved, That the next annual session of this Grand Lodge shall be held in the city
of San Francisco; and that San Francisco be selected as the site for the permanent location of the Grand Lodge of California.

All of which is respectfnUy submitted.
HUGH G. Pr.A.TT,
D. B. KURTZ,

1 Committee.
f

Which report was received and, with the resolution, was laid on the

.'lb tke M;·. W.'. Grand Lodge of California: The committee on Finance and Accounts have examined the books and

the following report: That they find there was on hand May 9th, 1853, a balance of cash, amounting to $2167 31, and the amount received from various
sources during the year aBd up to the present session (including the Sacra·
mento Relief Fund lis $6064 46, making a total of $8231 77.

which the Grand Treasurer has vouchers properly approved, is $3777 57,
leaving a balance in the hands of the Treasurer of $4454 20, from which,

The special committee appointed last year, to whom was referred the
communications relative to the Mount Vernon Orphan Asylum, and the
National ashington Monument, presented the following resolutions:----:-

'V

Resolved, That this Grand Lodge donate to the Orphan Asylum to be established at Mount Vernon, the sum of one hundred dollars, the same to be
forwarded to Bro. B. B. FRENCH, Grand Master of the District of Columbia,
whenever the Grand Lodge shall be informed that said Asylum is in progress
of erection.
Resolved, That a committee be appointed to procure a suitable block of
stone, with an appropriate inscription thereon, to be presented on behalf of
this Grand Lodge, to the National Washington Monument Association, with
a request that it be placed in a proper position in said Monument.
Resolved, That said committee raise, by voluntary subscription from the
Lodges under this jurisdiction, a sufficient sum to pay the expenses of the
same.
.T. D. CREIGH, Ol1aii'man.

RuLJC t. The examination of the charges shall be had in a ],odge especially
notified and convened for the purpose, at which_ no visitors shall be admitted,
except as counsel or witnesses.
ItuLE 3. The accused may seleci any brother for his counsel, and the witnesses shall testify, if :Masons, on their honor, as such. Hearsay evidence
shall be excluded.
Rur,1' 4. 'l'he question, "Is the accused guilty or not guilty?" shall be
distinctly put to each member of the Lodge, by name, commencing with the
youngest. The. answer shall be given standing, and in a distinct and audible
manner. The Secretary shall record the answer as given.
Ruu: :i. If the verdict be suspension or expulsion, the name of the person
so suspended or expelled shall be sent up at the ensuing meeting of this
Grand Lodge for examination : and if no appeal be taken. the action shall
be final.
UuLJC li. A sentence of expulsion shall not take effect until confirmed by
this Grand J,odge; but shall operate as a suspension of the delinquent, in the
mean time. In all cases a majority of two.thirds shall be necessary tu suspend or expel.
SmJ. 2. A suspensioll or expulsion of' a Brother frnm a Hoyal Arch Chapter, or an Encampment of Knights 'femplars, shall not operate as an expul~ion from the Lodge of which he i8 a mcmlier.

AH'l'ICLE VIII.
OF 'rHE GRAND LODG1'.
8EG'rlON. 1. The Grand Lodge has original and exclusive jurisdiction
over all subjects of Masonic legislation, and appellate jrisdiction only from
the decisions of the subordinate Lodges ; and its enactments and decisions
upon all questions shall be the supreme Masonic law of the State. It shall
also prescribe such rules and regulations for the government of the subordinate Lodges as will, in its ju<lgment, conduce to the welfare, prosperity, and
happiness of the craft; and may grant Dispensations and Charters for the
establishment of new Lodges, and may revoke and annul the same, for such
causes as it may deem sufficient. It shall prescribe the manner, and shall
require a uniform mode of working; and may require from the subordinate
Lodges such reasonable dues and fees, as will at all tin1es discharge the engagements of the Grand Lodge.

more than five candidates at any one meeting; nor shall more than one candidate be initiated, passed, or raised, at one and the same time.

SEC. 9. lt is not competent for a Lodge to expel or suspend ii member
from the rights and privileges of Masonry for the non-payment of dues;
but any member, who shall have neglected to make payment of his dues for
the space of six months, except in case of protracted illnes,g, or absence from
the State, may be summoned to appear before the Lodge to show cause why
his name shall not be stricken from the roll of members. And any Mason,
whose name shall be stricken from the roll, shall not be received as a member
of any other Lodge, nntil he shall have paicl np all arrearages to the Lodge of
which he was last a member, or, in the event of the 11on-existence of said
Lodge, to the Grand Lodge.
SEC. 10. All complaints and charges brought against a brother before a
Lodge, proper to he written, shall be reduced to writing, and filed with the
Secretary; a copy of which shall be sen·ed upon the accused at least ten days
before he is arraigned for trial. And, after trial had, should the accused be
suspended or expelled from the righw and privileges of Masonry, it shall be
the d.uty of the Lodge to report the fact to the Grand Lodge at its next stated
meetmg.
&Ee. 11. In all cases of suspensiou or expulsion by a subordinate Lodge,
the right of appeal lies to the Grand Lodge ; bnt no such appeal shall be
entertained unless prosecuted at the stated meeting of the Grand Lodge next
ensuing the time of tho decision from which the appeal is made; and no suspension or expulsion shall be published until the Grand Lodge shall have
cognizance thereat: 'l'he Grand Lodge shall prescribe rules and regulations
for the trial of all appealH.
c

taken to the body of the Lodge; but five members of any subordinate
Lodge may prefer charges to the Grand Master, or in his absence, to the
Deputy Grand l\Iaster, against the Master of 5aid Lodge, who, upon the
hearing of said charges, shall, if he deem necessary, suspend said Master
from the exercise of the duties of his office until the next Annual Grand
Communication.
SEc. 13. No l'jntered Apprentice or Fellow·Craft Mason, shall be adva11('ed
to a superior .degree, in any Lodge, other than that in which he received the
previous degree, unless by the consent, in writing, of that Lodge, if said

are present who voted on the question; nor shall it be in order unless moved
by one of the majority, and seconded.
. ART. 4. Every motion requiring to be reeorded shall be in writing. In
fillmg blanks, the largest sum or number and the longest time shall be first
stated.
ART. 5. If the previous question shall be called for by one Lodge and sc~onded,~y another, a?d the i:iajority sustain such call, it shall be put in this
torm: Shall Jb.e mam que.s~1on now be put?" If carried, such mam question
s.hall be the .ongmal P.ropos1t1on, as amended at time of adoption of the call
tor the previous question.
ART. 6. .No member shall speak more than twice on the same subjeot, unless he oJ:tam consent of the Grand Lodge.
ART. 1. All reports of committees shall be in writing.
ART. 8. Any of the above By-Laws may be amended or when necessary
suspended for the time being, on the concurrence of twd thlrds of the mem~
bers present.

Expulsion.- This is the highest penalty that <.:an be incurred, and the
severest punishment that can lJe inflicted, for any violation of Masonic en·
n·agements. It excludes a Mason from all his Masonic rights and privileges
for ever, unless he he restored by the Grand J,odge. It ought never to he
exercised bnt with extreme cauti011, and iu <:aHeR whc•re a lighter punishment,
would be of no avail.

A Notification Is the notice by which thi; time, phiee and hour, (and
frequently the business,) of the Lodge are communicated to the members.
This notice every brother receiving it, is expected to obey, unless his doing
so would materially interfere with his business engagements. 'l'he obligation
which it imposes is a general one ; aud the highest penalty incurred by a
violation of it, is forfeiture of membership.
A Sui11111011S- Is a call of authority ii eitatiou to appear and answer to
the charges therein set forth. Or, it is an imperative inJunction to appear at
a special meeting of the Lodge with which the . br?ther receiving it is affil.
iated ; or, to attend on the Grand Master, the Drntnct Deputy Grand Master,
or anv committee, or other body, authorized by the Grand Lodge to issue it.
The obligation to obey it is special and obligatory on every brother rec"living·
it. The penalty for its uon·observance is expulsion: unless the party offend.
in)!' he able to urge a pressinj! and positive uecessity for his excuse.
